# Open investigations

A checksheet, not a narrative. `governance-campaign-findings.md` records what was
found and why; this records **what has not been looked at yet**, so it does not
have to be rediscovered from a chat log.

Each item: what to check, why it might matter, and what would settle it. Status
is one of **open**, **in progress**, **parked** (deliberate, with a reason), or
**done** (move the finding to the campaign doc and delete the row).

**Status reconciled 2026-10-19 at `04a1e8c4`.** 41 rows changed. 30 had fallen
behind the history and now read fixed (A17 and A19 in part), or warned by
decision -- a fix landed
under another row's ID, inside a squash commit named for a sibling, or in the
row's own later text while the status cell said VERIFIED. 11 are still open and
had their evidence re-measured or corrected (A3a, A3b, A18, A21, A22, A28, F7,
F19, F23, F24, F30). Each changed row carries a dated note naming its evidence: the
fixing commit plus the registered test that pins it (traced), or a probe re-run
at that commit with a control (verified). Of the open rows NOT changed, these
were checked against the code they cite and still describe it (traced): A3a,
V3a, A3b, A4, A5, A8, A9, A29 (pinned arms pass), F3, F35/F36/F46 (pin suite
passes), F44, F49, F52, F53, B1, B6, B7, B10, B11, D10, D12, D14. A35, A37 and
A39-A45 rest on their recorded probes: none of the files they cite changed
after they were written, apart from #323's budget lines. NOT re-checked: A1 and
A30's inert-key half (sweeps of their own), the parked decisions B2-B4, C2-C4
(need a live run), D2-D8 (living-script harness history), D13, D15, D16
(toolchain and environment). Fixed rows are kept rather than deleted, as the
rows above already do; the delete rule above is not followed in practice, and is
left for the owner to settle.

---

## A. Inert mechanisms — the high-yield category

Two engine features have now been found **configured, believed working, and
structurally incapable of firing**:

- `CONTRA-007` iterated a set its own loader empties at parse time, so the
  conflict it looks for cannot exist by the time it runs.
- The circuit-breaker ladder's middle levels were gated by a *second* threshold
  (default 0.70) sitting above their own (0.4 / 0.6), so ELEVATED and HIGH could
  never be reached through pressure.

Both were found by accident, from a different direction. Neither was found by
review. That base rate is the argument for looking systematically.

**The two shapes to search for:**
1. a check reading state that a loader, normalizer or resolver clears first;
2. a gate whose counter/condition is fed by a threshold other than its own.

| # | Item | Status | How to settle it |
|---|---|---|---|

| R5 | **Make healing relative rather than absolute.** `coherence_natural_healing` is a per-turn constant, so whether it suppresses escalation depends on the workload's damage rate — measured cliff at 0.50 for one profile and 0.15 for a lighter one (**both re-measured 2026-08-25: neither reproduced — the cliff sat between 0.20 and 0.30 on two fresh profiles; see the R3 re-examination in `docs/proposal-bounded-coherence-healing.md`**). A rate capped at a fraction of the agent's own recent damage per damaging turn would self-scale and have no cliff. | **implemented (opt-in, default off)** | Implemented 2026-08-26; 11 gates pass in `tests/governance_v4/test_relative_healing.sh`. Verified with control: `f=0.5` returns a floored agent to 1.0000/normal, while the same fixture with no healing stays floored. Scale invariance measured directly — heal/damage-rate held at 0.500 across a 2.44x change in damage magnitude. **Two design corrections, both found by running it**: the `f < 1.0` safety clamp made the proposed positive control (suppression at `f=1.5`) unreachable, so it moved to absolute-0.30; and RH-02's original failure was the acceptance FIXTURE, not the code — S17 fires every recovery turn, and only a mechanism that out-heals a live signal passes it, which is suppression. See the implementation-outcome section of the proposal. Design written up in `docs/proposal-relative-healing.md` (2026-08-25), including the regression surface this row asked for. Core property is DERIVED, not measured: recovery time works out to `n_damaging / f` clean turns, so the damage magnitude cancels and there is no cliff to find; `f < 1` is the safety invariant. The trace turned up one fact that shapes the design — damage is booked POST-CLAMP, so a floored agent books zero further damage and the rate window must span damaging turns only, or it decays to zero and recreates the trap. Gates RH-01..RH-08 specified, with RH-05 (suppression reappears at f>=1.5) as the positive control without which the no-suppression claim is unfalsifiable. |

Worth deciding whether the denominator should be the prompt's keyword count, or whether the threshold should scale with mandate length. |

### A2 traced properly: the `limits.*` family, key by key

The screening sweep said "77 unread". Tracing each key to the point of
enforcement — rather than to the point of *reference* — gives a different and
much more useful answer. Three distinct things were being conflated.

**Reference is not reachability.** Six check methods read a `limits.*` key and
have **zero call sites**: `getMaxLinesForLanguage`, `getTimeoutForLanguage`,
`checkNestingDepth`, `checkOutputSize`, `checkStringLength`, `checkDictSize`
(whose only apparent caller is a *comment* in `governance_config.cpp` recording
that it has none). A grep for the key finds the read and reports the key live.

**A similarly-named sibling is not the same key.** `max_lines_per_block` appears
enforced at `governance_checks.cpp:1447` — but that is
`code_quality.max_complexity.max_lines_per_block`, not
`limits.code.max_lines_per_block`. This is the third time in this campaign that a
name collision has produced a wrong reading (`max_tokens_per_run` vs
`max_total_tokens` killed two runs; `forward_batch_size` vs `batch_size` nearly
put six working keys on a defect list).

**An inert knob is not an absent protection.** Most `limits.*` keys sit on top of
enforcement that works, driven by a different source:

| key | protection | driven by |
|---|---|---|
| `limits.memory.total_mb`, `per_block_mb` | RLIMIT_AS / Job memory, real | the **sandbox level**, hardcoded (128 MB at RESTRICTED) — governance never sets it |
| `limits.data.array_size`, `dict_size`, `string_length` | throws on overflow, real | `include/naab/limits.h` **compile-time constants** (10M / 1M / 100MB) |
| `limits.data.nesting_depth` | parse depth bounded | `MAX_PARSE_DEPTH`, compile-time |

`limits.memory.total_mb` is the sharpest case and corrects an earlier claim in
this document. The loader *does* read it (`governance_config.cpp:447`) into
`rules_.memory_limit_mb`, which is why it looked wired — but `getMemoryLimitMB()`
has no callers, and `SubprocessContainment::max_memory_bytes` is populated from
`ScopedSandbox::getConfig().max_memory_mb`, never from governance. The chain runs
three links and dead-ends on the fourth.

**Live from govern.json**, verified by tracing to a reachable enforcement site:
`limits.execution.loop_iterations`, `limits.execution.polyglot_blocks`,
`limits.rate.max_polyglot_per_second`, `max_stdlib_calls_per_second`,
`max_file_ops_per_second`, and `limits.data.output_size` (via `polyglot.cpp:718`,
not via the dead `checkOutputSize`). Since A18 (2026-10-20) `checkOutputSize()` enforces it on both engines,
and the `polyglot.cpp:718` throw is gone.

**A fourth conflation, and the one that produced the most wrong answers: the
loader MIRRORS nested keys into flat legacy fields, and the flat field is what
enforcement reads.** `limits.execution.call_depth` sets `rules_.max_call_depth`;
`limits.data.array_size` sets `rules_.max_array_size`; `limits.timeout.global`
(line 791) sets BOTH `rules_.timeout_seconds` and `rules_.runtime.timeout`, and
`main.cpp:1913` enforces the latter. The nested key is the modern spelling, the
flat field is the original, and a scan that excludes the loader sees the modern
name written and never read — so a wired key reads as dead. Two entries were
wrongly listed here as unenforced on exactly this basis, including
`limits.timeout.global`, which had been singled out as *the* aggregate an
operator would most reasonably assume they had. They have it.

(Note one quirk found while confirming it: `main.cpp:1913` guards on
`rules.runtime.timeout != 30`, a sentinel against the default, so configuring the
global timeout to exactly 30 seconds is a no-op.)

**Genuinely unenforced, with no protection behind them by any route** — nine
keys, and the only ones that warrant a wire-or-delete decision:

    limits.code.max_functions
    limits.code.max_variables
    limits.code.max_total_polyglot_lines
    limits.code.max_nesting_depth
    limits.execution.total_executions
    limits.execution.parallel_blocks
    limits.timeout.total_polyglot
    limits.data.input_size
    limits.rate.cooldown_on_limit_ms


#### The nine, traced individually — and only two are truly uncovered

Tracing each of the nine to the thing it would protect, rather than stopping at
"nothing reads this key", collapses the list again. Two corrections to the
framing above come first:

**"The engine never accumulates" was wrong.** `limits.execution.polyglot_blocks`
is an aggregate over the whole run, it is LIVE, and it is backed by a member
counter (`polyglot_block_count_`, incremented by
`incrementAndCheckPolyglotBlockCount()`). The per-item/aggregate split was a tidy
story that the code does not support.

**Parallel polyglot execution is real**, not a phantom feature —
`polyglot_async_executor.h`, `executePolyglotGroupParallel()`,
`group.parallel_blocks`.

| key | what actually protects it |
|---|---|
| `limits.execution.total_executions` | **redundant** — `limits.execution.polyglot_blocks` is live and counts the same executions |
| `limits.execution.parallel_blocks` | **inert knob** — concurrency is bounded by a hardcoded `ThreadPool(2)` in `polyglot_async_executor.cpp` |
| `limits.timeout.total_polyglot` | **subsumed** — the whole-run timeout (`limits.timeout.global` → `runtime.timeout` → `main.cpp:1913`) already bounds it |
| `limits.data.input_size` | **inert knob** — `limits::checkStringSize` enforces `MAX_INPUT_STRING` (100 MB) at `lexer.cpp:69` |
| `limits.code.max_nesting_depth` | **covered** — `MAX_PARSE_DEPTH` (1000) enforced at `parser.cpp:18`; the scanner also has `deep_nesting` |
| `limits.code.max_total_polyglot_lines` | **partly covered** — per block by `MAX_POLYGLOT_BLOCK_SIZE` (1 MB) and the live `code_quality.max_complexity.max_lines_per_block`; the program-wide total is unbounded |
| `limits.rate.cooldown_on_limit_ms` | **nothing, and nothing to wire it to** — no cooldown or sleep exists anywhere; the live rate limits block rather than throttle. Its default is `100`, so an operator sees a plausible non-zero value for behaviour that does not exist |
| `limits.code.max_functions` | **nothing** |
| `limits.code.max_variables` | **nothing** |

So the honest count is **two** keys where an operator gets no protection and
nothing else covers it, plus one (`max_total_polyglot_lines`) whose aggregate
half is uncovered.

`max_functions` looked cheap to wire because `profile.function_count` already
exists — but that is computed **per polyglot block, by regex over the block's
source** (`syntactic_analyzer.cpp:83-90`), for complexity scoring. It is not a
program-wide count of NAAb functions, so wiring the key to it would enforce
something other than what the key's name promises. There is no `variable_count`
field at all.

**Recommendation, revised by the trace**: delete `cooldown_on_limit_ms`,
`total_executions`, and `total_polyglot` as redundant or unimplementable-as-named;
leave `parallel_blocks`, `input_size` and `max_nesting_depth` alone but document
them as knobs over hardcoded protections (the same treatment `trust_policy` got);
and treat `max_functions` / `max_variables` as the only genuine wire-or-delete
decisions — noting that wiring them needs a program-wide counter that does not
exist today, for a limit whose value nobody has ever been able to rely on.


##### The three delete candidates, checked before deleting

Deleting is the one recommendation that removes something, so each was traced to
the mechanism it would attach to. Two survive as deletes; the third does not.

**`limits.rate.cooldown_on_limit_ms` — delete, and the reason is structural.**
`RateLimiter` (`governance.h:2663`) is a fixed-window counter: `max_per_second`,
`window_start`, `count_in_window`, and a `check()` returning bool. There is no
field a cooldown could live in and no sleep or backoff anywhere in the tree. More
decisive than the missing wiring: on breach the callers **throw**
(`polyglot.cpp:173`), so the run ends. A cooldown is only meaningful for a
limiter that *waits*; you cannot cool down from a thrown error. Wiring this key
would not be connecting a wire, it would be replacing fail-fast with throttling.
Its default is `100`, so an operator sees a plausible value for a design that was
never built.

**`limits.execution.total_executions` — delete, redundant.** The claim was
`limits.execution.polyglot_blocks` covers it, and the trace makes that stronger
than assumed: `incrementAndCheckPolyglotBlockCount()` is called from **six**
sites spanning both engines — `vm.cpp`, `polyglot.cpp` (twice),
`call_dispatch.cpp`, `modules.cpp` — *and* `codegen_impl.cpp:360`. So the live
counter already counts every dynamic code execution including codegen, which is
exactly what "total executions" would mean.

**`limits.timeout.total_polyglot` — do NOT delete. The earlier "subsumed"
verdict was wrong.** Polyglot time is bounded at two scales, and neither is the
one this key expresses:

* per block — `max_cpu_seconds` from the **sandbox level** (hardcoded, 10 s at
  RESTRICTED), applied as `RLIMIT_CPU` and read by `js_executor.cpp:114`;
* whole run — `limits.timeout.global` → `runtime.timeout` → `main.cpp:1913`.

Cumulative time *in polyglot specifically* — "this script may run for five
minutes but must not spend more than thirty seconds in embedded code" — is
expressible by neither. The only substitute is indirect: block count (live) times
the per-block CPU bound, and the operator controls only the first, since the
second is fixed by sandbox level. So this key names a real and otherwise
unexpressible constraint. Deleting it removes intent; leave it, and either wire
it or document it as unimplemented.

Noted in passing at `main.cpp:1913`: the global timeout is applied as
`std::max(cli, config)`, so `--timeout` can **extend** a govern.json limit. The
comment says that is deliberate, but it makes the governance timeout a floor
rather than a ceiling, which is the opposite of how every other governance limit
reads.


###### The final two, exhaustively

Both were the last candidates standing after four narrowings. Tracing them
completely removes one and reclassifies the other.

**Lifecycle, both keys.** Declared once (`governance.h:270-271`, default `0`),
parsed once each (`governance_config.cpp:874-875`) with an `explicitly_set`
entry, merged across `extends` (`4594`, `4602-4605`), written into every
generated config by `naab governance init` (`max_functions: 100`,
`max_variables: 500`), and shipped in **both** copies of `govern-template.json`.
Read by nothing.

They are also **less** plumbed than their dead siblings, which is itself a
signal. The `clamp0` block covers 21 limits fields and omits both; the ratchet
covers `max_total_polyglot_lines` and `max_nesting_depth` and omits both. So a
negative value would survive parsing unclamped, and neither participates in
mid-run tightening checks.

**`limits.code.max_functions` — delete. It is redundant with a live check.**
The scanner has `function_count` (`checks_complexity.cpp:101-109`): a configurable
`max` (default 25), enabled at advisory level by the generator, reported as a
proper scanner issue with a remediation hint. It is not a dormant tool —
`main.cpp:2128` runs a **preflight scanner on every governed execution**, over the
main file *and* its imported modules, before execution so advisories survive an
exit-3 block. So the capability the dead key names is already delivered, by a key
that works, at file granularity, with better reporting. This is the same
similarly-named-sibling shape that has now produced a wrong reading five times in
this campaign.

**`limits.code.max_variables` — no equivalent anywhere, and no definition.** The
scanner's complexity family counts cyclomatic, cognitive, file length, functions,
classes, imports, returns and nested ternaries — **there is no variable count**.
Nor is there one in the analyzer profile (`syntactic_analyzer.h:18-40` has
`function_count`, `loop_count`, `max_loop_depth`, `max_function_depth`, and no
variable field), nor in the interpreter (`values_.size()` is one Environment's
scope map, a runtime binding count per scope, not a program total).

It is therefore the single key in the whole sweep that expresses something
nothing else expresses. That is not the same as a gap worth filling, because
**nobody can say what it should count**: static `let` declarations? runtime
bindings? peak live bindings across scopes? A loop declaring one `let` over a
thousand iterations is one declaration and a thousand bindings, and the two
readings differ by three orders of magnitude. The template ships `500`, a number
with no definition behind it.

**Wiring cost, if it were wanted.** Governance's static checks take
`const std::string& code` (`governance.h:2823-2829`) — **source text, not an
AST** — so any count enforced by the engine is necessarily a regex, with the usual
miscounting inside strings and comments. The AST-accurate route does not exist
either: `TypeChecker` visits every `FunctionDecl`, but `main.cpp:1699` runs it
only under `--strict-types` or `--verbose`, so a limit hosted there would not
apply to a normal run at all.

**Both were deleted.** Removed from the struct (`governance.h`), the parser and
the `extends` merge (`governance_config.cpp`), the generator
(`governance_init.cpp`) and all three operator-facing templates
(`govern-template.json`, `docs/govern-template.json`,
`docs/book/verification/govern.json`). Test fixtures under `tests/gorilla`,
`tests/e2e` and `tests/llm-ab-testing` still carry the keys; unknown keys are
ignored silently, so they are inert data, and rewriting 30+ scenario configs
would risk unrelated breakage for no behavioural gain.

`test_inert_key_sweep.sh` caught the removal on the first run — 77 entries to 75,
naming both keys — which is the guard doing exactly its job in the *good*
direction. Its failure text said "now enforced", which is wrong for a deletion;
it now names both possibilities and asks which. Baseline updated to 75.

**On whether these were ever wired**: this clone's history is shallow — 112
commits beginning 2026-07-22 — so `git log -S` reports every one of the dead
check methods as first appearing in the second visible commit, which means only
that they predate the visible history. Whether enforcement was removed at some
point cannot be answered from this repository, and no claim either way should be
made from it.

Note what unites them: every one is an **aggregate** — a total across the
program, a whole-run timeout, a cumulative count — while every live limit is
**per-item**: one block, one value, one call, one second. The engine enforces
what it can decide at a single point and does not accumulate. That is a coherent
architectural boundary rather than scattered rot, and it means "wire them up" is
not a small change: it requires state that does not exist today.

Two further layers: the signal also **defaults to `false`**, and `agent.environment()` therefore reports `capabilities_granted` as an **empty list for every agent that has ever run**. | open | **Proven with a positive control**, not inferred. Same fixture throughout — a tool calling `env.get` at turn ≥10, tool executing 25 times: stock build → 0 firings; a scratch build seeding `granted_capabilities` → fires at turn 12; seeded build with the capability exercised from a *script* instead of a tool → 0, which separately confirms B6 blocks the script path. **The first attempt at this control FAILED and the failure was the useful part**: the signal defaults to false, so my fixture never enabled it and I had been reading a structurally-inert harness as a fact about the engine ("a sweep over explicit values cannot see a default", `investigation-method.md`). Pinned by `tests/governance_v4/test_signal_reachability.sh` SR-02/SR-03, with SR-04 as the load-bearing control — it asserts `scope_creep` DOES fire on the same fixture, without which SR-02's silence is equally satisfied by "tool events never reach CDD at all". Fix is not obviously additive: populating the set turns a signal ON for every existing config, so it needs the same deliberate call as the A2 keys. **Status precisely, 2026-09-06 — the MECHANISM shipped in #196; the DECISION did not.** `initializeGrantedCapabilities()` is now called unconditionally at `current_turn == 0` (`agent_impl.cpp:1810`), and SR-02/SR-03 assert S7 fires on a capability first used at turn>=10 and that `agent.environment()` reports `capabilities_granted` populated. So S7 is no longer inert. It stays `open` because `capability_underutilization` defaults **false** (`governance.h:1478`) — flipping that default is the behaviour-changing half, and it is the same call the A2 keys await. Do NOT read "#196 fixed A4" as "A4 is done": that conflation was made once in this campaign and had to be withdrawn against this row. |

All six `CHECK_FAILED` emission sites (`agent_impl.cpp:3669, 3687, 3715, 3726, 3738, 3766` — the `shell_blocked` and `blocked_path` response-scan restrictions) **throw within three lines**, and `checkContextDrift` is further down the same straight-line function (`:4065`); the only intervening catches are `regex_error` at `:3685`/`:3724` and a `catch (...)` at `:3882` scoped tightly around `nlohmann::json::parse`. So the turn that produces a `CHECK_FAILED` never reaches `recordTurn`, and turn-stamping keeps that event out of every later bucket. On the stock build the same shape gives 10 sends and 9 `CDD_TURN` events. **Read the COUNT, not the turn index**: `CDD_TURN`'s turn field runs one ahead of `RESPONSE_SCAN`'s, and reading the index is exactly how this was briefly and wrongly written off as "the ordering claim collapsed" — the blocked attempt appears as two consecutive `AGENT_RESPONSE` events with no scan and no CDD between them. Fix direction unclear: emitting the event before the throw is not enough on its own, because the turn still has to reach `recordTurn`. **That sentence is now out of date and is kept only to show what the fix had to get past — 2026-09-06.** #196 added `recordBlockedCapability()`, which records the blocked attempt so a RETRY arms the signal without the blocked turn ever needing to reach `recordTurn`. SR-07 asserts S4 fires after a block when the capability is retried, with SR-08 as the control (the identical fixture without the retry stays silent). | A7 | **The Python polyglot import blocklist is bypassed by object-graph traversal, and the static regex that would catch the naive form is off by default and token-based.** The embedded (in-process pybind11) executor blocks modules by monkeypatching `builtins.__import__` (`python_c_executor.cpp`, the `_naab_safe_import` hook, active whenever `languages.per_language.python.imports.blocked` is non-empty). Reaching a module through the class graph — `"".__class__.__base__.__subclasses__()` walked to `BuiltinImporter`, then `.load_module("os")` — never calls `__import__`, so the hook never fires. | **VERIFIED, then FIXED (runtime audit hook)** | Confirmed by execution 2026-09-07 on this build (embedded executor live — object files present, 65 `PythonCExecutor` symbols, python block returns 5 not null). Three layers traced to the point of effect, not accepted from the report: **(1) Import hook** — POSITIVE CONTROL: literal `import os` is HARD-blocked (exit 3, `languages...imports.blocked`); the subclass-traversal reaches `os` and runs `os.getcwd()` **and** `os.open`/`os.read` of `/etc/hostname` (returned `vm`), governance **PASS exit 0**, the byte-identical file read via literal `import os` is exit 3. Hook bypass real. **(2) Static scanner** — the report claimed "no static regex catches it"; that is WRONG. `PYTHON_EVAL_CHECKS` (`governance_checks.cpp:4986`) carries `__subclasses__\s*\(`, and with `code_quality.semantic_checks.enabled: true` it HARD-blocks the payload (positive control: a bare `().__class__.__subclasses__()` trips the same pattern). BUT `SemanticChecksConfig.enabled` defaults **false** (`governance.h`) — a distinct key from `imports.blocked`, so the first, plausible config (blocklist on, semantic_checks unset) has NO scanner and the escape works. Sweep absent, not false. **(3) Both are token gates and fall together to obfuscation** — POSITIVE for the failure: with semantic_checks ON and the full blocklist, `getattr("".__class__.__base__, "__subcla"+"sses__")()` and `getattr(_imp,"load_"+"module")("os")` reaches `os.getcwd()`, governance **PASS exit 0**. The regex cannot see a token built at runtime and the hook never sees an `import`. Root cause: the embedded executor has only source-level (regex) and import-level (Python monkeypatch) gates — no syscall/audit sandbox — so object-graph + runtime string construction defeats both. `sys.addaudithook` is the right SHAPE (fires on the syscall regardless of spelling) but is a real change with its own false-positive surface, not a one-liner, and is deliberately NOT shipped in this verification pass. Fixtures under `/tmp/claude-0/a7*` (not committed). **Scope of what was tested:** embedded executor only (this build). The bypass is language-level (pure object graph), so the import-hook half is executor-independent by construction; the subprocess-fallback executor additionally carries `SubprocessContainment` (RLIMIT_NPROC=0) which blocks fork/exec but NOT an in-process `os.open`/`os.read` — reasoned, not measured. **Sibling claims from the same report, still screened-not-verified:** `checkPolyglotOutput` runs only after the child exits (`polyglot.cpp:1911`) so a filesystem write lands before its non-JSON stdout is refused — the gate stops the RESULT being parsed, not the ACT; and S10 Jaccard is defeated by prepending the prior turn to a malicious payload. **FIXED 2026-09-08 (runtime audit hook).** The root cause was that a polyglot block is the one I/O path in the tree with no NAAb call site — every other path asks the sandbox (`file.read`->`canRead`, `http.get`->`canConnect`, `process.run`->`canExecuteCommand`), but once control enters CPython there was nowhere to put the question, so governance was source-text-only and lost to object-graph traversal by construction. Fix installs `PySys_AddAuditHook` before `Py_Initialize` (`python_c_wrapper.c`) and routes the `open`/`os.system`/`subprocess.Popen`/`os.exec*`/`socket.connect` audit events to a C++ policy (`naabPythonAuditPolicy` in `python_c_executor.cpp`) that asks the SAME `ScopedSandbox::getCurrent()` the rest of the codebase asks. The hook fires at the OPERATION regardless of how the module reference was obtained, so it closes the cached-`sys.modules` bypass the import blocklist could never see. Verified: obfuscated escape (`getattr(x,"__subcla"+"sses__")`, no literal token) reading `/etc/hostname` under `filesystem.mode:none` now raises `PermissionError` at `os.open` BEFORE any fd is returned — the read never happens — while a python block importing stdlib still runs (runtime-path carve-out: reads under `sys.path` are the interpreter loading itself, allowed; load-bearing because `mode:enforce` upgrades the sandbox to `standard`, which refuses absolute paths). Test: `tests/governance_v4/test_python_audit_hook.sh`, AH-02 load-bearing (obfuscated read no source gate can see), with a documented negative control (disable the registration -> AH-02 fails with the exact A7 symptom `READ=vm`/PASS/exit 0). **Residual scope, stated:** covers file/exec/network via the standard audit events; a block that makes RAW syscalls via `ctypes` (which raises `ctypes.dlopen`/`ctypes.call_function` events, not covered here) is a deeper layer and remains open. The refusal surfaces as a catchable `PermissionError`, which is correct: the syscall is denied at the boundary so a NAAb `try/catch` around the block gains no data by catching it — prevention, not just detection. |

So the ordering is right — governance is consulted on every call — but the ceiling it is consulted against is fixed: an operator who configures `call_depth: 2000` gets `runtimeError("Stack overflow")` at frame 1024 and the configured gate is never reached. Its severity was also overstated as a hard crash: `runtimeError` is an ordinary catchable VM error, not a fault. **Not verified by execution** — building a >1024-deep call chain and reading the exit path is the missing step, and it needs the under-1024 case as its positive control. Fix direction if confirmed: either size `frames_` from `max(1024, rules().max_call_depth + 1)` at construction, or reject a configured `call_depth >= FRAMES_MAX` at config load, which is the smaller change and fails loudly instead of silently. Note the tree-walker reaches `checkCallDepth` from `call_dispatch.cpp:44` with no `FRAMES_MAX` analogue at all, so the two engines do not share this ceiling — untested, and a candidate divergence. |

So a mid-run reload can flip it in either direction and the one-way ratchet says nothing. | **verified (absence traced in both directions); fix deliberately deferred** | Found by an outside agent's defaults audit and then re-derived here rather than inherited — the parse site and the two sibling ratchet sites were each read directly. **The reason this is not a one-line addition is that the flag loosens in BOTH directions, so "which way does the ratchet point" has no obvious answer.** Turning it ON absorbs statistical penalties inside the baseline window, which is straightforwardly less enforcement. Turning it OFF silences S17 entirely (`persona_baseline_mean` is gated on `baseline_complete`, which is set only inside the adaptive block — see C1e) and freezes S12's EMA at its opening window (`behavioral_sequence.cpp`), so an operator disabling it removes one signal outright and pins another. | A10 | **`path.*` stdlib touches the filesystem with no sandbox call site — the same structural class as A7.** `src/stdlib/path_impl.cpp` has **zero** references to `ScopedSandbox`/`canRead` (traced). `path.exists` calls `fs::exists` and `path.resolve` calls `fs::canonical` directly. Note the fix is NOT the `canRead` gate this row proposed: the sandbox is an allowlist with no concept of `blocked_paths`, so a `canRead` call would not have delivered the project path policy. Original evidence: Reported by an outside agent (Gemini batch, Finding 14); re-traced and re-run here rather than inherited. Under `capabilities.filesystem.mode: none`: POSITIVE CONTROL `file.exists("/etc/shadow")` is HARD-blocked (exit 3, `Filesystem access is not allowed`); PROBE `path.exists("/etc/shadow")` returns **true**, `Governance: PASS`, exit 0. Severity is BELOW A7: this is information disclosure (existence, plus canonical/symlink-target resolution via `path.resolve`), NOT content read or write — but it is a real filesystem-probing primitive that ignores policy. **This is the A7 CLASS, not a one-off**: a stdlib module with no sandbox call site. A7 (polyglot), A10 (path) are two confirmed instances; a systematic audit of every stdlib module for a `ScopedSandbox::getCurrent()` gate is the real fix, and is larger than patching `path_impl`. Fix for this row alone: add `canRead` gates to `resolve`/`exists` (and any function that stats the filesystem) mirroring `file_impl.cpp`. Not fixed here — recorded with its control so the next pass starts from evidence. |
| A11 | **The SSRF private-host filter is bypassed by non-dotted-decimal IP notations.** `isPrivateHost()` (`http_impl.cpp:28`) gates on `inet_pton(AF_INET, host, ...)`, which parses ONLY strict 4-octet dotted-decimal. Shorthand (`127.1`), octal (`0177.0.0.1`) and 32-bit integer (`2130706433`) forms all FAIL `inet_pton` -> `isPrivateHost` returns false -> the SSRF gate is skipped, while curl resolves every one of them to `127.0.0.1`. | **fixed** (#229) | Reported by an outside agent (Gemini batch, Finding 15); trace and run reproduced here. `capabilities.network.enabled: true`: POSITIVE CONTROL `http.get("http://127.0.0.1:9")` -> `[SANDBOX VIOLATION] SSRF: private/reserved IP blocked`, request refused. PROBE `http.get("http://127.1:9")` -> NO SSRF block; the request reaches curl, which returns `Couldn't connect to server (7)` — i.e. it attempted the connection to loopback rather than being gated. The absence of the block message that the control produced is the proof. Real loopback/metadata SSRF bypass (169.254.169.254 has integer and octal forms too). Fix direction: resolve the host to its canonical numeric form BEFORE the range checks (or reject any host that curl would accept but `inet_pton` rejects), and add a redirect-verification callback — `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION=1` (line 265) follows 302s with no re-check, a second bypass in the same function noted by the reporter and not separately run here. Not fixed here. **UPDATE — FIXED by #229, incidentally and then pinned.** Measured on the same build, same endpoint: on master `127.1`, `2130706433` and `0177.0.0.1` each RETURNED the loopback secret while the dotted-quad `127.0.0.1` spelling of that identical address was blocked; with #229's `CURLOPT_OPENSOCKETFUNCTION` gate all three are refused. Cause: `isPrivateHost()` parses with `inet_pton`, which accepts ONLY dotted-quad, while `getaddrinfo` also accepts short/integer/octal — so the string check concluded "not an IP literal, therefore not private" and the resolver then connected to 127.0.0.1. The socket gate never sees the spelling, only the resolved `sockaddr`. Pinned by `SS-05` in `tests/security/test_ssrf_redirect_dns.sh` (per-form, each with a resolver control that reports UNMEASURABLE rather than passing on a platform whose `getaddrinfo` rejects the form). **UPDATE — FIXED by #229 (`87c9041`).** Enforcement moved to `CURLOPT_OPENSOCKETFUNCTION`, which libcurl calls with the RESOLVED `curl_sockaddr` once per socket — the initial request AND every redirect hop — so neither a redirect, a DNS record, nor an alternate IP spelling can reach a private address. Pinned by `tests/security/test_ssrf_redirect_dns.sh` (7 arms, incl. SS-04 the over-block control and SS-05 per-notation). **Status reconciled 2026-10-19 at `04a1e8c4` (traced):** #229 (`87c90418`) moved the SSRF decision to `CURLOPT_OPENSOCKETFUNCTION`, which sees the RESOLVED address, so `127.1`, `2130706433` and `0177.0.0.1` are judged as loopback. Pinned by `tests/security/test_ssrf_redirect_dns.sh` SS-05 (the URL-module path by SI-05, #315), which passed in the full suite at `b510fa98`, whose engine source equals `04a1e8c4`. |

The `agent.create()` runtime (`agent_impl.cpp`) never sets it; a runtime handle is tracked in `current_agent_handle_`, which those gates never consult. | **fixed** (#211) | Reported by an outside agent (Gemini Finding 16), but its test was a MISFRAME — it had the ORCHESTRATION SCRIPT read the file after `agent.create()`, and the script is not the agent (it runs as `anonymous`), so that read SHOULD be governed by the global sandbox, not the created role. The security-meaningful test is a TOOL the agent invokes: `restricted_agent` with `blocked_paths:[secret]` + a registered tool that reads it, driven by a stub tool-call. Result: `TOOL_SUCCESS=true`, secret returned, `Governance: PASS`. POSITIVE CONTROL: `naab run --agent-id restricted_agent` doing the same `file.read` is HARD-blocked (exit 3, `agents.restricted_agent.blocked_paths`). What is inert is the per-role FILESYSTEM scoping (blocked_paths, FS_READ/FS_WRITE matrix) for tool actions — you cannot give agent A a narrower file scope than agent B via the agents block and have it bind A's tools. Fix direction: resolve the acting role from `current_agent_handle_` (AgentTracker knows the handle->role map) into an effective id the gates consult, falling back to `agent_id_`. Blast radius is real — every agent tool action would newly enforce role scoping, which can break configs that rely on today's behaviour — so it wants observe-first, not a silent flip. Not fixed here. **Status reconciled 2026-10-19 at `04a1e8c4` (traced):** #211 (`b2140ba2`) routes the per-agent gates through `effectiveAgentId()`, so a tool callback is judged under the acting handle's role. Pinned by `tests/governance_v4/test_agent_role_binding.sh` AB-01..03 (AB-02 no-leak, AB-03 no over-block), which passed in the full suite at `b510fa98`, whose engine source equals `04a1e8c4`. |
| A13 | **`--sandbox-level` escalates the sandbox above the configured level, and `integrity.blocked_flags` cannot lock it — two compounding gaps.** (1) The blocked-flags preflight (`main.cpp:~1475`) calls `checkBlocked()` on a HARDCODED set of five flags (`--no-governance`, `--tree-walk`, `--drift-baseline-save`, `--governance-override`, `--governance-baseline-save`). `isBlockedFlag()` checks the full configured list, but nothing calls it for any OTHER flag, so a flag the owner lists in `integrity.blocked_flags` that is not one of those five is never consulted. (2) The configured `security.sandbox_level` is applied only `if (sandbox_level == "unrestricted")` (`main.cpp:1918`) — i.e. only when NO `--sandbox-level` flag was passed. Passing `--sandbox-level elevated` sets it non-unrestricted, so the configured level is skipped and the CLI value wins; the fail-closed clamp is likewise gated on `sandbox_level_config.empty()`, so it doesn't fire when a level is configured. | **VERIFIED + FIXED** | Reported by an outside agent (Gemini Finding 13); traced and run here. POSITIVE CONTROL that flag-locking is LIVE: `blocked_flags:["--governance-override"]` + `--governance-override` -> `INTEGRITY BLOCK`, exit 3. BASELINE: configured `sandbox_level: standard` blocks `file.read("/etc/hostname")` (absolute path), exit 1. PROBE: same config with `blocked_flags:["--sandbox-level"]` and `sandbox_level: standard`, run `--sandbox-level elevated` -> NO integrity block AND `READ_OK=vm`, exit 0: the flag lock was inert and the sandbox escalated standard->elevated. Tested UNSIGNED (empty trust store); the escalation half is signing-independent (pure CLI-vs-config precedence), and for SIGNED configs the implicit-block list covers only `--no-governance`/`--governance-override`, so `--sandbox-level` is not implicitly blocked there either (traced, not separately run) — arguably worse, since a signed config is exactly the locked case. Fix direction, two parts: (a) iterate ALL `integrity.blocked_flags` against argv rather than a hardcoded five; (b) make `--sandbox-level` tighten-only — CLI may only make the sandbox MORE restrictive than the configured level, never exceed it (same ratchet principle as `--timeout` taking the max). Not fixed here. **UPDATE — FIXED.** Both halves, in `main.cpp`. (a) The level predicate was `sandbox_level == "unrestricted"` — "apply the configured level only when NO `--sandbox-level` was passed" — so passing the flag skipped the block and the CLI out-ranked govern.json. It is now a rank comparison: the configured level wins whenever it is AT LEAST AS STRICT, leaving a stricter CLI value in place. Tighten-only, mirroring the `--timeout` rule three lines away in the same file (`std::max`, so the flag "can extend but not shrink the govern.json limit"). Byte-identical when no flag is passed, since the `"unrestricted"` default ranks least strict. Applied at BOTH sites — `applyGovernanceSandbox()` and the VM inline block — which the existing comment warns must agree or `test_sandbox_engine_parity.sh` fails. (b) Both preflights now sweep `argv` against the operator's configured `blocked_flags` (matching `--flag` and `--flag=value`) instead of five hardcoded names; the hardcoded calls stay as defence in depth, harmless when they duplicate a hit since the block is a latch, and an empty entry matches nothing as in the path and host policies. **The row understated the second half:** there are TWO preflights with DIFFERENT hardcoded lists (the second omits `--tree-walk`), so which flags were lockable depended on which execution path ran. Pre-fix control on the unfixed binary: exactly SP-02 and SP-03 fail while SP-00, SP-01, SP-04 and SP-05 pass; 6/6 after. SP-05 is the load-bearing arm — the CLI must still be able to TIGHTEN below the configured level, and without it a fix that merely ignored the flag would pass every other arm while deleting a legitimate facility. Note the first draft of the suite was WRONG and its own baseline caught it: the fixture lived in `/tmp`, which the `standard` sandbox allowlists, so it never discriminated `standard` from `elevated` and four arms failed where the defect accounts for two; `/etc/hostname` was verified across all four levels before being trusted. Test: `tests/security/test_sandbox_level_precedence.sh`. |
| A14 | **`blocked_paths` is unreachable for any path under an `allowed_paths` directory — allow beats deny with no specificity check.** In `checkPathAccess` (`governance_engine.cpp:~1975`), Layer 1 sets `explicitly_allowed=true` on any `allowed_paths` prefix match, and Layer 2 (`blocked_paths`) is wrapped in `if (!explicitly_allowed)`. So a broad allow silences a MORE-SPECIFIC block. The code comment states the intent — "specific allow like './data/' beats broad deny like '/'" — but it implements ANY allow beating ALL blocks, never comparing prefix length, so the common secure pattern (allow the workspace, deny `workspace/.env`) is defeated. | **fixed** (#224) | Reported by an outside agent (Gemini Finding 9); traced and run here. POSITIVE CONTROL: `blocked_paths:[secret.key]` with NO `allowed_paths` -> HARD block, exit 3. PROBE: add `allowed_paths:[workspace]` alongside `blocked_paths:[workspace/secret.key]` -> `SECRET=...` read, `Governance: PASS`, exit 0. The block is silenced by the broader allow. (NB: an earlier note here claimed I had brushed this myself; that was a DIFFERENT mechanism — the A7 Python escape hitting the sandbox `canRead` under `elevated`, not this `checkPathAccess`/`blocked_paths` precedence — so F9 rests on this direct test, not that observation.) Fix direction: longest-prefix-match wins — when both an allow and a block match, the more specific (longer canonical prefix) decides, so a specific block inside a broad allow is honoured while a specific allow inside a broad deny still works. Not fixed here. **Status reconciled 2026-10-19 at `04a1e8c4` (traced):** #224 (`fb1e4bd1`) put precedence in one place, `decidePathAccess()`: longest matching prefix wins, ties deny. Pinned by `tests/security/test_path_precedence.sh` (PP-02 is the arm that stops "deny always wins"), which passed in the full suite at `b510fa98`, whose engine source equals `04a1e8c4`. |

PROBE B: `metadata.google.internal` listed in `blocked_hosts` -> also reaches curl, no block. POSITIVE CONTROL that pre-curl network gating EXISTS and works: A11's `127.0.0.1` is refused with `[SANDBOX VIOLATION] SSRF` before any connection — so the reached-curl outcome here is meaningful, not just an absent gate. **Severity nuance:** the metadata IP `169.254.169.254` is still covered by A11's SSRF link-local range check (by IP), so blocked_hosts being unread does not by itself open the metadata IP; the real loss is `allowed_hosts` — an operator cannot restrict an agent's egress to a domain set, so data exfiltration to an arbitrary host is ungated by this control (global `network.enabled:false` still works). Fix direction: copy `rules.capabilities.network.allowed_hosts`/`blocked_hosts` into `SandboxConfig` in `syncGovernanceToSandbox`, and add a `blocked_hosts` deny check to `canConnect` (resolve-then-check to avoid the A11 numeric-form bypass class). Not fixed here. **Status reconciled 2026-10-19 at `04a1e8c4` (traced):** #235 (`eb8438ee`) found the sandbox had always implemented host allowlisting and the config never populated it; it now does, adds `blocked_hosts`, one matcher, deny wins. Pinned by `tests/security/test_network_host_policy.sh` NH-00..06, which passed in the full suite at `b510fa98`, whose engine source equals `04a1e8c4`. Still outside it: embedded Python (A35), subprocess languages (CONTRA-013), and a mid-run reload (host lists reach the sandbox at startup only). |
| A16 | **Inert filesystem/process capability knobs — parsed (some ratcheted) but never enforced, so they invite operator false-confidence.** LOWER severity than A10-A15: these do not OPEN a hole, they are cosmetic switches that do not do what their name says; the real controls live elsewhere. **`capabilities.process`** — the parser (`governance_config.cpp:781`) reads ONLY `parseRationale`; `spawn`/`signals`/`max_processes`/`allow_daemon` are never extracted and never read (Gemini Finding 11). **`allow_hidden_files`/`allow_absolute_paths`** — parsed (`:739-740`) AND ratcheted (`:3542-3543`) and emitted by `naab governance init`, but zero enforcement reads (Finding 12). **`blocked_extensions`** — emitted by init (`governance_init.cpp:416`) and declared (`governance.h:168`) but NOT parsed at all, so even setting it does nothing. | **warned at load, not enforced -- by decision (#241)** | F11 PROBE: `process.spawn:false`+`max_processes:0`, `process.run("echo")` -> `RAN=hi`, PASS, exit 0. F11 CONTROL: `capabilities.shell.enabled:false` -> `[SANDBOX VIOLATION] SYS_EXEC`, exit 1 — proving process spawning IS gatable, just not by the `process` fields (`capabilities.shell` is the real gate). F12 PROBE: `allow_hidden_files:false`, `file.read(".hidden_secret")` -> `HID=...`, PASS, exit 0. The exposure is FALSE CONFIDENCE, not a new bypass — an operator who sets `process.spawn:false` or `allow_hidden_files:false` believes spawning/dotfiles are blocked when they are not. Fix direction: either enforce them (wire `process.*` to the exec gate; add hidden-file/extension checks to `checkPathAccess`) or reject them at load with an "unenforced key" error rather than silently accepting — the ratchet on the two fs bools makes silent acceptance worse, since it implies they matter. Not fixed here. **Status reconciled 2026-10-19 at `04a1e8c4` (traced):** #241 (`b6d56ca3`) WARNS at load when one of these keys requests enforcement, and deliberately does not wire them: the template ships `signals: false` and `allow_daemon: false`, so enforcing would start blocking configs that pass today. A scan, not a regex: a candidate regex for these forms crashed on a 200 KB argument (observed, libstdc++ recursion). `block_system_info_leak` reaches `process.run` too. Before (observed): `process.run("ps", ["aux"])` and `<<shell>> ps -A` ran under the restriction. | A18 | **`limits.data.output_size` (polyglot output cap) is unenforced on the default VM engine and CATCHABLE on the tree-walker.** The check lives only in `polyglot.cpp:718` (tree-walk path); `vm.cpp` has ZERO references to `output_size`/`max_output_size`, so under the default VM engine the cap never runs. Where it does run (tree-walk), it is a plain `throw std::runtime_error` (`polyglot.cpp:720`), not `enforce()`, so NAAb `try/catch` swallows it — violating the same enforce-vs-throw discipline CLAUDE.md documents for GovernanceHardError. | **fixed** 2026-10-20 | Reported by an outside agent (Gemini Finding 7). `limits.data.output_size:20`, python block returning 100 bytes: VM (default) -> `LEN=100`, `Governance: PASS`, exit 0 (silent). POSITIVE CONTROL `--tree-walk` -> `polyglot output exceeds configured limit`, exit 1 (the cap is real on that engine). F7b: `--tree-walk` with the block wrapped in NAAb `try/catch` -> `SWALLOWED ... SCRIPT_CONTINUED`, exit 0 — the throw is catchable. Net: an agent produces unbounded polyglot output past the configured cap on either engine (silently on the default one). Same engine-divergence shape as the taint/BSD gaps: a check wired into one engine's polyglot path and not the other. Fix direction: move the output-size check to a shared post-execution governance call reached by BOTH engines, and raise it via `enforce()` (uncatchable at HARD) rather than `throw`. Not fixed here. **Status reconciled 2026-10-19 at `04a1e8c4` (traced):** unchanged -- see A3a. The uncalled `checkOutputSize()` (A3b) is the VM-side check this row lacks. **Fixed 2026-10-20.** History (traced): the VM was the default engine by `94058845` (2026-03-28); the cap arrived in `cc975e33` (Finding G, 2026-04-04) in the tree-walker's sequential path only, as a plain throw that predates `GovernanceHardError` (`fd97d0ae`, 2026-06-06); `src/vm/` never mentioned it; the `enforce()` version, `checkOutputSize()` (`bcf647a0`), never had a caller. No commit records the VM as left out on purpose. The row was narrower than the defect (observed at `a1252222`): on the tree-walker a javascript block escaped the cap too, because javascript takes the parallel-group path (`executePolyglotGroupParallel`), which had none -- the python block this row used as its control takes the sequential path; a LIST holding the same bytes escaped both engines, since only a string result was measured; and persistent runtimes' `.exec()`, `codegen.run()` and block-library calls were never capped. Fix: `checkOutputSize(language, result)` (and a byte-count overload for codegen's output string) enforces at HARD through `enforce()`, like every `limits.*` check, and counts a list or dict without building its text. Every route calls it: VM inline blocks and runtime `.exec()`; the tree-walker's sequential and parallel paths, runtime `.exec()` and five block-library call sites; `codegen.run()`. The VM's three block-call sites are wired but unreachable today (its compiler refuses `use BLOCK-...`). No level key, matching the siblings; the caps stay where they were in govern.json. Behaviour change, tightening: a project made by `naab-lang init` or from the template has `output_size: 1000000`, so a polyglot result over 1 MB is now a HARD block on the VM, as it already was (catchably) on the tree-walker's sequential path. Test: `tests/governance_v4/test_polyglot_output_cap.sh` (34 pass, 1 UNMEASURABLE -- the VM block arm; on a binary built from this tree with the engine change backed out, every control passed and every defect arm failed, 15/17). Revert-checked, one mechanism removed at a time (observed): counting strings only -> OC-03 on both engines; the VM inline site -> OC-01/02/03/04/05/09 on the VM; the VM runtime site -> OC-06/vm; the tree-walker parallel site -> OC-01..05/tw; its sequential site -> OC-09/tw; its runtime site -> OC-06/tw; the codegen site -> OC-07 on both; the block-library helper -> OC-08/tw; the per-language half -> OC-04 on both; a plain string instead of `enforce()` -> every global-cap refusal arm, OC-04 staying green. Each removal turned only its own arms red. |

F18: PC `<<python hashlib.md5>>` blocked, PROBE `crypto.md5("test")` -> digest, exit 0. F19: PC `<<python base64.b64encode>>` blocked, PROBE `crypto.base64_encode("password123")` -> `cGFzc3dvcmQxMjM=`, exit 0. F21: `http.get("http://...")` with `https_only:true` -> reaches curl (no scheme block; A11's SSRF block is the pre-curl positive control that gating exists). **This is now the dominant pattern in the batch** (A7 polyglot, A10 path, A17 env, + F17/F18/F19/F21) — the fix is architectural: policy must be enforced at the stdlib call site / a shared governance hook, not as a source-text scan that only sees polyglot blocks. Not fixed here. **Status reconciled 2026-10-19 at `04a1e8c4`:** F17 and F18 fixed by #232 (`7e91ee65`; NS-01..05 and NS-10..13), F21 by #235 (`eb8438ee`); their suites passed in the full suite at `b510fa98`, whose engine source equals `04a1e8c4`. **F19 re-probed and still open (verified):** under `restrictions.data_exfiltration` with `block_base64_encode_secrets`, the control `<<python base64.b64encode(...)>>` is refused (exit 3) while `crypto.base64_encode("password123")` in the same config prints `cGFzc3dvcmQxMjM=` (exit 0); a run with no restriction is the can-run control. Probe authored for this reconciliation, not committed. **F19 fixed (Bundle 3, 2026-10-27):** `crypto.base64_encode`/`base64_decode` answer to `block_base64_encode_secrets` and `hex_encode`/`hex_decode` to `block_hex_encode_secrets`, at the restriction's level (`checkNativeEncodingAllowed()`, the F18 shape), and only where the built-in source patterns apply: operator `patterns` replace them for source, and they are regexes a native call has nothing to match against. Red first on the pre-fix binary (observed: NE-01..04/06/08 failed, controls passed). Test: `tests/governance_v4/test_native_encoding_exfil.sh` (9 arms; 3 mutants). |
| A20 | **`checkPrivilegeEscalation` regexes miss octal SUID and hyphen-less `su` — a source-regex completeness gap (and source-text-only).** `block_su` = `\bsu\s+-` and `block_chmod_suid` = `chmod\s+[ugo]*s`. | **VERIFIED + FIXED** | Traced. `su root` (no hyphen) and octal `chmod 4755` bypass, as reported. BUT the reporter also listed `su -l` and `su -c` as bypasses — those DO match `\bsu\s+-` (the `-` follows), so that part is WRONG; only the hyphen-less and octal forms escape. Same class as F8a (regex completeness) AND, being source-text-only, also bypassed wholesale by native `process.run("su",...)` per A19/F17. Fix direction: match octal SUID (`chmod\s+[0-7]*[4-7][0-7]{3}`) and `su` without requiring a trailing hyphen; but recognise source-text is the weaker mechanism — the real fix is gating at the exec call site. Not fixed here. **UPDATE — FIXED.** Both flags now do what their names say, in `checkPrivilegeEscalation()`. `block_su` was `\bsu\s+-` and required a HYPHEN, so `su - root`/`su -l`/`su -c` were caught while `su root` and a bare `su` ran free; it now also matches `su <user>` and a trailing bare `su`. **The row UNDERSTATED the second half: `block_chmod_suid` caught NOTHING AT ALL**, not merely the octal forms. The pattern was `chmod\s+[ugo]*s` — against `chmod u+s` the class takes `u`, the pattern then needs a literal `s` and finds `+`; backtracking to empty needs `s` and finds `u`. No match, and the same for `+s`, `ug+s` and every octal mode. So the SYMBOLIC forms it was presumably written for were missed too: an inert sub-check, not a partial gap, which matters because an operator setting the flag got nothing whatsoever. Now `chmod\s+[ugoa,]*\+s` plus `chmod\s+0?[2-7][0-7]{3}\b`. **Two constraints on the fix.** Bounded patterns only — each is a character class followed by literals, never `.*` between two required literals, the shape that made SECRET_PATTERNS exhaust the stack and kill the interpreter; a crash renders NO verdict, strictly worse than the false negative it replaces. And THREE-DIGIT modes stay unmatched — `chmod 755`/`644` are ordinary and a check that blocks them is one that gets switched off, after which it catches nothing; `1755` (sticky only) is not escalation and also stays unmatched. Pre-fix control: exactly the seven THE-FIX arms fail while all ten controls pass, including four false-positive arms (`chmod 755`, `chmod 644`, `sudoku`, `issue`) that pass BEFORE the fix on purpose. 17/17 after. **Scope deliberately not taken, measured not assumed:** `pkexec id`, `doas id` and `setcap cap_setuid+ep` also ran free. Genuine escalation, but new command FAMILIES — folding them into `block_sudo` would change what that flag means and could block configs that pass today. Left for a separate decision. Test: `tests/security/test_privilege_escalation_patterns.sh` (17 arms). |
| A21 | **Tree-walk taint launder: an inline polyglot expression passed directly as a sink argument is treated as untainted.** `expressionContainsTaint()` (`governance_taint.cpp:149`) omits `ast::InlineCodeExpr`, so `file.write("leak.txt", <<python "untrusted" >>)` under `--tree-walk` sees a clean argument, while the VM taints polyglot output unconditionally (`vm.cpp:3165`). | **verified 2026-10-19; unreachable under enforce since Bundle 2 (`--tree-walk` is refused, exit 4: `tests/cli/test_tree_walk_enforce.sh`); open under audit and off** | This is a specific instance of the general gap CLAUDE.md already documents: *`expressionContainsTaint()` returns false for any node type it does not handle, so a new value-producing `Expr` subclass silently launders taint until added.* `InlineCodeExpr` is exactly such a node on the tree-walk path. NOT independently re-run here (VM is the default engine and taint parity is covered by `test_taint_engine_parity.sh`); recorded so the InlineCodeExpr case is named. Fix direction: add `InlineCodeExpr` (and audit the other value-producing Expr nodes) to `expressionContainsTaint`. Cross-ref B11 for the broader tree-walk BSD/taint-emission divergence. **Status reconciled 2026-10-19 at `04a1e8c4` -- now measured (verified):** under `taint_tracking` (source `polyglot_output`, sink `file.write`, hard), `--tree-walk` with the block INLINE as the sink argument wrote the file (exit 0), while the same value through a variable on the tree-walker, and the inline form on the VM, were each refused (exit 3). `expressionContainsTaint()` still has no `InlineCodeExpr` case (`governance_taint.cpp:131`). A taint bypass on the tree-walker only. Probe authored, not committed. |
| A22 | **BSD is blind to `env.get_all()` (no ENV_READ event), and the sliding-window `max_gap` drops partial sequences — one real gap, one by-design.** (a) `vm.cpp:1944` emits `ENV_READ` only for `env.get`/`env.list`, NOT `env.get_all()`, so a secret-harvest-then-exfil pattern that reads via `env.get_all` never registers a step. (b) inserting `> max_gap` benign events between two pattern steps flushes the partial match. | **(a) fixed 2026-10-20 / (b) by design** | (a) is the SAME blind spot as A17/F8b — `env.get_all` is the ungoverned env-dump primitive, here on the BSD event-emission side rather than the disclosure-scan side; it should emit `ENV_READ` like its siblings. Reported probes (env.get_all then http.get -> 0 violations) are consistent with the trace; not independently re-run. (b) the `max_gap` window drop is the DOCUMENTED behaviour of the sliding-window detector (`behavioral_sequence.cpp`), a deliberate bound on how far apart pattern steps may be — widening it trades false negatives for cost/false positives, a tuning decision, not a defect. Fix for (a): add `env.get_all` to the `ENV_READ` emission site. Not fixed here. **Status reconciled 2026-10-19 at `04a1e8c4` -- (a) now measured (verified):** with a hard pattern `[env.get, file.write]`, `env.get` then `file.write` is refused (exit 3) and `env.get_all()` then `file.write` runs (exit 0); both VM emission sites still name only `env.get`/`env.list` (`vm.cpp:1926`, `:1994`). #232 closed the disclosure-scan side (F8), not this event side. Probe authored, not committed. **Fixed 2026-10-20, and the sweep found the class wider than the row.** History: BSD's stdlib classification was two hand-written name lists in `OP_CALL_METHOD` -- the pre-execution check (`b9a201fc`, then `09c2228c` added `file.read_lines` to it only, `62c35490` `agent.send`) and the post-call emission (`e928c1a7`) -- and the zero-argument member route had neither, although `3984fe9b` (Finding C/D, April) had already named that route as needing OP_CALL_METHOD's governance mirrored into it; BSD came a month later and reached one route. Measured at `2e9c120d` through the engine's own event stream (`--event-stream-fd`), one program per call (observed): of 36 calls only 12 recorded an event, one of them falsely (`process.getpid`, below). Unseen: the two true aliases `env.get_all` (the same branch as `env.list`) and `env.set` (the same branch as `env.set_var`); `env.get_int/get_float/get_bool`, `env.delete_var`, `env.load_dotenv`, `file.write_lines`, `io.read_file/write_file`, `csv.read/read_dict/write/write_dict`; `file.read_lines` (pre-checked, never emitted -- see A50); and `env.get_all` with no parentheses (A51). `process.getpid()` recorded a `process.exec` event through a `"process."` prefix rule, so `env.get` then `process.getpid()` completed a `[env.get, process.exec]` hard pattern (observed: exit 3). One table now classifies every function of ten modules (`include/naab/bsd_stdlib_events.h`; env, file, io, csv, http, process, crypto, path, log, agent -- 112 functions), read by both VM routes; pre-checking is decided by event TYPE, so two names of one event cannot disagree again, with the previous type set kept (ENV_WRITE and DECODE recorded after the call only). `tests/self-audit/test_bsd_event_coverage.sh` fails when a covered module's `hasFunction()` names a function the table does not list. Test: `tests/governance_v4/test_bsd_stdlib_events.sh` (45 arms; on `2e9c120d` every control passed and every defect arm failed, 18/27). **Behaviour change, both directions:** each newly recorded call can now complete a pattern (tightening), and each new event also spends every pattern's `max_gap` budget, so a sequence with several `io.read_file` calls between its steps can now fall outside a gap it used to fit -- consistent with the same program written with `file.read`, which already behaved so. `process.getpid` and `process.exit` no longer record (`process.run` and `process.kill` do): the one removal, of a false event. **Still open, recorded rather than decided:** (i) `file.copy`/`move`/`delete` record nothing -- the detail carries only the FIRST argument, so a copy's destination could not be matched and its source would be; (ii) whether an environment write should be refused before it happens (ENV_WRITE is still not pre-checked); (iii) an env dump's detail (`env.get_all`) cannot satisfy a secret-name glob, so `credential_harvesting` (`env.get:*KEY*` ...) does not fire on a dump followed by a send -- a dump reads every secret, so whether it should match is a decision; (iv) a call that throws records nothing (a refused `http.get` is no NET_CONNECT), as before; (v) traced, not measured: `bolo.load_config(path)` opens a file that neither this table nor the filesystem gate's allowlist (`filesystemAccessMode`) classifies. The tree-walker records no BSD event for a stdlib call at all (B11). **Decision analysis 2026-10-20 (traced; nothing changed).** (i) Never built: `e928c1a7`'s list named none of the three. Correct: a copy or move records a FILE_READ of the source and a FILE_WRITE where the file lands (`copyMoveDestination()`, the path gate's own resolution); a delete records a FILE_WRITE, as the filesystem gate already classifies it. No new event type, so existing step names keep working. (ii) `b9a201fc` names its criterion ('abort dangerous actions ... before callStdlibMethod() executes'); nothing records why DECODE and ENV_WRITE were left out. A decode is pure, and at HARD a match after the call ends the run before the value is used, so post-call is enough. An environment write lands in the process before the post-call match, and the `on_violation` hook that match fires inherits the program's environment (traced, `fireHook()`; see A53), so ENV_WRITE should be pre-checked. (iii) User patterns can already name a dump (`test_bsd_stdlib_events.sh` B-01/B-02). For the built-in name globs: a dump reads every variable it returns, so it should satisfy a glob when a returned name matches; the names must travel with the event (including the MCP `--event-stream-fd` record, or cross-call replay diverges) but stay out of telemetry. (iv) A refused call should record nothing: the refusal is its own evidence, and a refused action must not complete a pattern. A call that acted and then failed (a POST that sent its body) should record. None of the four is a govern.json setting: patterns are already configurable, and a switch that turns event coverage off would be one more way to loosen policy. |
| A23 | **URL module import (`import "http://host/mod.naab"`) performs raw network egress and remote code execution with ZERO governance.** `ModuleResolver::resolve` sends `http://`/`https://` specifiers to `downloadUrl()`, which runs libcurl (`curl_easy_perform`) straight to a disk cache with no gate. Bypasses `network.enabled:false`, `allowed_hosts`/`blocked_hosts` (already inert per A15), `https_only` (already inert per A19/F21), the A11 SSRF check, AND the sandbox `NET_CONNECT` capability — then parses and EXECUTES the downloaded code. | **VERIFIED, then FIXED (#213)** | Reported (Gemini F25). Structurally the most severe in the batch: it is remote code ingestion, not just egress. Traced to `module_resolver.cpp` downloadUrl → curl with no `checkNetworkAllowed`/`canConnect`. NOT independently run here yet — it needs standing up a loopback HTTP server, and the A11 SSRF gate would block loopback for `http.get` but this path does not even reach that gate. Fix: `checkNetworkAllowed(host,port)` + `canConnect` + `https_only` scheme check before `curl_easy_perform`, and ideally block URL imports unless a capability explicitly permits them. Highest-severity open item — recommend prioritising the empirical confirmation and fix. **UPDATE 2026-10-14 — #213 fixed the literal spellings only.** Measured on `526044de`: `import "http://127.0.0.1:P/m.naab"` refused, the same module imported and ran from `127.1`, `2130706433` and `0x7f000001` -- #213 added a URL-STRING check, and #229's resolved-address gate reached `http.get` only. Now shared (A34). |
| A24 | **`codegen.run_strict` is omitted from the taint bridge, so a tainted code string it executes bypasses `codegen.allow_tainted_code`.** Both taint-bridge sites — `vm.cpp:1926` and `call_dispatch.cpp:1512` — match `mod == "codegen" && (method == "run" || method == "run_with_args")`, calling `setCodegenArgTainted(...)`. `run_strict` is not in the match, so `t_codegen_arg_tainted` stays false and the `allow_tainted_code:false` gate never sees the taint. | **VERIFIED, then FIXED (#213)** | Reported (Gemini F26). Traced to the exact two match sites; `run_strict` is a documented codegen entry point (throws on non-zero exit, catchable) and is simply absent from the taint dispatch. Not independently re-run (the trace is a literal missing method in a boolean match). Fix: add `|| method == "run_strict"` at both sites — the smallest fix in the batch, and worth doing alongside A25/A23 as they share the codegen/module ingestion surface. |
| A25 | **Local `import "path.naab"` bypasses filesystem governance — the A10 class on the module path.** `ModuleResolver::parseModuleFile` reads via `naab::readFileBounded(path)` (`module_resolver.cpp:419`) with no `checkPathAccess`/`checkFilesystemAllowed`/sandbox `canRead`. Neither the AST import evaluator, VM `OP_IMPORT_MODULE`, nor the resolver consult governance, so `capabilities.filesystem.mode:none` and `blocked_paths` do not bind module reads. | **VERIFIED, then FIXED (#213)** | Reported (Gemini F27); traced and run. Under `filesystem.mode:none`: POSITIVE CONTROL `file.read("secret_module.naab")` -> HARD block, exit 3; PROBE `import "secret_module.naab" as sec` -> reads, parses, runs `sec.leaked()` -> `MODULE_SECRET_LEAKED`, `Governance: PASS`, exit 0. Same class as A10 (path)/A7 (polyglot) — a read path that never reaches the sandbox call site. Fix: `checkPathAccess(resolved_path, "read")` in `parseModuleFile`/`resolve` before reading. Note the fix must run on BOTH engines (VM `OP_IMPORT_MODULE` and the tree-walker import evaluator) or it reproduces the B11-class engine divergence. |
| A26 | **REST `/api/v1/execute` kills the whole daemon on any HARD governance block — remote DoS.** `rest_api.cpp:161` catches `GovernanceHardError` in the HTTP worker and calls `_exit(3)`, terminating the process instead of returning an HTTP error. | **fixed** (#223) | Reported (Gemini F28). Ran `naab-lang api`; a single POST of `file.read("/etc/hostname")` under `filesystem.mode:none` (a HARD block) took the server down — the next request returned connection-refused (`http_code=000`). The `_exit` is deliberate on the CLI (main.cpp catches HARD and `_exit(3)`s the process, correct there), but the same handler is reused in a long-lived server worker. Fix: in the REST catch, `res.status = 403` + a JSON error body and `return`, never `_exit`. High severity — unauthenticated single-request DoS if the API is exposed. **UPDATE — FIXED by #223 (other session), not by this campaign.** Re-verified independently on `eacaaa4` rather than inherited: a `<<python>>` write POSTed to `/api/v1/execute` under `sandbox_level:"restricted"` returns `python execution denied by sandbox` and **the server is still ALIVE afterwards**. The `_exit(3)` is gone from all three sites; the handler now sets `exit_code = 3` and answers the request. Their `tests/security/test_entry_point_parity.sh` EP-03 pins it. **Status reconciled 2026-10-19 at `04a1e8c4`:** status cell aligned with the UPDATE above; `tests/api/test_rest_hard_block_survives.sh` passed in the full suite at `b510fa98`, whose engine source equals `04a1e8c4`. |
| A27 | **The REST API installs no sandbox, so sandbox-gated stdlib ops run unrestricted — remote code execution at the daemon's privilege.** The CLI wraps execution in `ScopedSandbox script_sandbox(security_config)` (main.cpp:1369); `rest_api.cpp` instantiates ScopedSandbox NOWHERE (0 occurrences), so `ScopedSandbox::getCurrent()` is nullptr on every worker and the stdlib gates take their `if (!sandbox) allow` fallback. **Important nuance:** the governance ENGINE *is* active on the REST path (the interpreter loads govern.json; `checkSecrets`/`checkFilesystemAllowed` fire — which is how A26's HARD block triggers), but the SANDBOX layer (`canExecuteCommand`, `canConnect`, `canRead`) is absent. So it is not "no governance" — it is "no sandbox". | **fixed** (#223) | Reported (Gemini F29). POSITIVE CONTROL (CLI): `process.run("touch", [marker])` under `capabilities.shell.enabled:false` -> `[SANDBOX VIOLATION] SYS_EXEC`, marker NOT created. PROBE (REST): the same code via `/api/v1/execute` returned `status:success` AND **created the marker file** — process execution actually happened despite shell being disabled. Fix: instantiate a `ScopedSandbox` synced to the request's SecurityConfig around the execution block (mirror main.cpp's sync), so the sandbox-gated ops bind on the REST path too. Highest network-reachable severity in the batch. **UPDATE — FIXED by #223 (other session).** Re-verified independently on `eacaaa4`: the same REST probe is refused at the SANDBOX layer and **writes no file**, so the entry point now establishes process policy. Note `rest_api.cpp` still contains zero `ScopedSandbox` references — the fix works by establishing the policy process-wide rather than per handler, so grepping that file still reads as though the hole is open. EP-02 pins the refusal and **EP-04 is the load-bearing arm**: it asserts a PERMISSIVE rule is also honoured at both doors, without which a build that refused everything would report perfect parity. **Status reconciled 2026-10-19 at `04a1e8c4`:** status cell aligned with the UPDATE above. |
| A28 | **`naab.toml` manifest security/language controls are inert — parsed then discarded.** `main.cpp:1372` does `auto manifest = ManifestLoader::findAndLoad(".")` with the comment "configuration will be applied by interpreter", but the local is used ONLY for `has_value()` and a version `fmt::print`; it is never passed to Interpreter, VM, Compiler, SandboxConfig, or GovernanceEngine. | **reported (Bundle 3); not applied, by decision** | Reported (Gemini F30). Traced every use of the `manifest` local — none reaches an enforcement path; the reassuring comment is exactly the "a confident comment is not a verified one" case from investigation-method. So `[polyglot] python = false` / `[features] sandbox_mode = true` in `naab.toml` do nothing. Not independently re-run (the trace is decisive — there is no call site to exercise). Lower severity than A26/A27 (false confidence, not a live network hole), but it is a documented project-level control that enforces nothing. Fix: wire the manifest into GovernanceEngine/SandboxConfig at load, or reject unenforced manifest keys rather than accepting them silently. **Status reconciled 2026-10-19 at `04a1e8c4` (traced):** unchanged -- the `manifest` local in `main.cpp` (`:1475`) is still used only to print package fields. **Bundle 3 (2026-10-27), by the plan's decision "no second policy source":** the keys stay unapplied, and a value that asks for something NAAb does not do -- a `[polyglot]` language set false, `[features] sandbox_mode = false` -- is reported on every run and by `naab-lang manifest check`, naming the govern.json key (`languages.blocked`, `security.sandbox_level`); the default manifest's comment no longer says the section enables runtimes. Test: `tests/cli/test_manifest_unapplied.sh` (6 arms; MF-04/05 controls; 3 mutants). |
| A29 | **`agent_review`: all 13 template settings are LIVE, but five of them fail open or go silent on some input, and the template's own section cannot run.** Swept 2026-10-03 with stub agents: `tests/governance_v4/test_agent_review_settings.sh` sets each setting against unset (plus `hints`, which the loader reads and the template omits), on both engines. Each setting's set arm goes red when its consumer is neutralised in source, the unset arms of the six defaulted keys go red when the loader default is flipped, every pinned arm goes red when what it pins is "fixed", and every arm goes red against a dead interpreter (kill tables in the suite header). Open, all in the false-reassurance direction: **(a)** `fail_policy` blocks only on the exact string `"closed"`; `"CLOSED"` and every other value fail OPEN with no warning (AR-12d). **(b)** `enforcement.<zone>` recognises only `"hard"`/`"soft"`; any other value (`"block"`, `"error"`, and `"detect"`, a documented enforcement tier, all probed) enforces ADVISORY while the banner prints the configured string, e.g. `green/block`, exit 0 (AR-10d). `"HARD"` was in that list until level names became case-insensitive (2026-10-09, project owner's decision); it now blocks like `"hard"` (AR-10e). Unset is advisory in every zone, red included (AR-09u). **(c)** under `dispatch_mode: "parallel"` + `fail_strategy: "continue"`, a failed agent is reported only when EVERY agent failed — one success drops the failure silently and `fail_policy: "closed"` does not fire (AR-15e). Under `"sequential"` (the default and the template's value) `fail_strategy` is never read (AR-15d) — a conditional inertness the leaf-name sweep in `test_inert_key_sweep.sh` cannot represent, since the key IS read on the parallel path. **(d)** `enabled: true` with no `detection` list reviews nothing and prints nothing (AR-02c); a `scorer` naming no `scorers` entry falls back silently (AR-06c). **(e)** The template's section names agents (`redteam`, `testing`, `analyst`, `voice`) its own `agents` block does not define (`researcher`, `coder`), and `"scorer": "security"` does not match the template's scorer key `security_review` (verified: giving `security_review` a loader-read key changes the outcome only when referenced by that exact name). Flipping `enabled` on the section verbatim runs nothing and warns once, because the template's `fail_policy` is `"open"`. Reachability: nothing loads either template copy and `naab-lang init` emits no `agent_review`, but `init` prints the template as the "Full reference", so it is what an operator copies. Reach of the phase itself (traced, not run): only the two CLI run-path sites in `main.cpp` call `runAgentReview()`; `rest_api.cpp` has none, so a script submitted to REST `/execute` is never reviewed whatever the config says. Related, for the `scorers` sweep: the template's `scorers.security_review` body (`name`, `description`, `weights.*`, `thresholds.*`) uses no key the loader reads (`governance_config.cpp` reads `enabled`, `default_weight`, `rule_weights`, `*_threshold`, `threshold_mode`) — traced, and consistent with the verbatim arm above behaving like the default scorer. | **(a)/(b) fixed, (c) reported (Bundle 3); (d)/(e) open** | Each of (a)-(d) is pinned by a `PINNED, not endorsed` arm, so a fix flips a named arm instead of passing unnoticed. Decide per item: (a)/(b) warn or refuse unknown values at load (the `capabilities.functions.level` precedent warns and disables; for a fail-closed switch, refusing is the safer reading); (c) surface partial failures and let `fail_policy` see them; (d) warn at load; (e) fix the template's names, and settle the `scorers` body in that section's sweep. **Bundle 3 (2026-10-27):** (a) `fail_policy` is compared without case, and a value other than open/closed is treated as closed with a load warning (the fail-closed reading this row proposed; tightening only). (b) An unknown zone level is hard with the warning every level-only key prints; `detect` and `approval_required` block as hard (the review runs before the program, where nothing catches a block); an explicit `advisory` stays advisory (AR-10g). (c) A failure `continue` proceeds past is now printed; whether `fail_policy: "closed"` should then block is still a decision (AR-15e pins it). Test: `test_agent_review_settings.sh` AR-10d/10f/10g/12d/12e/15e (51 arms). |
| A30 | **`govern-template.json` did not show what the loader reads, and put some of what it did show where the loader never looks.** An alias-aware walk of `loadFromJson()` (`tools/template_coverage.py`, authored; self-test controls per access shape) found 147 read leaves absent from the root copy after filtering 104 `parseEnforcementLevel` level-string artifacts and 44 per-block `rationale` keys (documented once by `_comment_rationale`) — all of `circuit_breaker.output_admissibility`, `deescalate_sustained`, `level_effects`, the S20–S23 signals, `telemetry.decision_snapshots`, `agents.<name>.api_base`, and more. Nine keys sat at paths nothing reads: `subprocess_scrub_mode` and the three subprocess var lists at the TOP LEVEL (read only under `capabilities.env_vars`; the schema validator lists them as valid top-level keys, so nothing warned), `code_quality.project_intent`/`function_intents` beside `intent_validation` instead of inside it, `drift_detection.semantic_checks`/`min_lines_for_check`, and `behavioral_sequences.cross_agent` (a per-pattern key). All nine came from `cb42af9b` ("sync with parser"), whose own loader already read the correct paths. The docs copy had also drifted from the root one and shipped S8–S11 as `false`, against an engine default of `true`. | **template fixed; inert keys open** | 84 of the 147 are now in both copies (now byte-identical), each at its struct or loader default with a `_comment`. Eight of the nine misplaced keys were moved to the path the loader reads, with their true defaults rather than the example values they carried (the top-level `blocked_subprocess_prefixes: ["AWS_", ...]` would have started scrubbing for anyone copying it). The ninth, `drift_detection.semantic_checks`, was removed and is described in a comment instead: `code_quality.semantic_checks` is presence-enabled, and even its scalar default `false` has its own `explicitly_set` entry, which `mergeRules()` uses to stop an `extends` parent's enabled checks being inherited. Verified with a `<<sh>>` child echoing a planted variable: the top-level keys leave it set, exactly like a config with no policy (the matched control), while the same keys under `capabilities.env_vars` remove it.
