| A65 | **A VM `async fn` ran with no VM-side governance.** *(Numbered A59 in PR #329's commits; renumbered because open PR #333 also adds an A59.)* Found 2026-10-21 by the A64 sweep: `saveCounterState` (the tree-walker's V-GOV-004 counter hand-off) was executed by no unit, and probing it showed the tree-walker held while the VM did not count at all. `VM::callValue()` runs each `async fn` call in a fresh `VM async_vm`; `5ce3c20c` gave it the engine, `4f65b2b0` (2026-04-19, an async segfault fix) removed it on purpose ("shared governance/module_resolver mutated concurrently"), and #269 later propagated only the thread-local engine pointer for the embedded-Python hook. Verified at `dd7744fd`, each config refusing the same call in main{} at exit 3, inside an async fn on the VM: a `blocked_paths` file read printed the file (exit 0); a `restrictions.code_injection` `os.system` in a `<<python>>` block ran and wrote its marker (exit 0); `limits.execution.polyglot_blocks` 2 let three blocks run. The tree-walker refused all three. | **fixed** | The async VM gets the engine again. Answering `4f65b2b0`'s reason: ThreadSanitizer on eight concurrent async fns, each making 20 rounds of `file.read`, `env.get` and a `<<sqlite>>` block, reported one race in NAAb state, the return-taint flag (now `thread_local`, with its source), and none in three runs after (observed: 4 warnings before, then 2, 0, 0, every one after in libstdc++). Every check in that program PASSED; the block count is one atomic increment. libstdc++'s `std::ctype<char>::narrow` cache, which concurrent `std::regex` compiles write with identical values, is reported and left (not NAAb state). **A second program, written to put the VIOLATION path on several threads** (taint at advisory, so a violation records and continues: 8 async fns x 20 `env.get` -> `file.write`), found two more, both fixed here. (1) **The VM's sink check for a tainted argument raced** (observed): it marked a fixed label (`argument 1 of 'file.write()'`, `(print-arg)`, a module-call argument) in the engine's shared taint set, called `checkTaintedSink()`, which looks the name up, and cleared it -- three lock acquisitions on a name every thread uses, so another thread's clear landed between this thread's mark and lookup and the write reached its sink unchecked: 155-160 of 160 violations recorded, all 160 in 4 of 19 runs, while one async fn and main{} recorded 160 every time, DETECT level caught all 160 in 5 of 5, and an occurrence count proved `enforce()` was never called for the missing writes. TSan saw nothing, since every step held the lock. The VM now passes the taint it knows (`taint_stack_`) to `checkTaintedValueAtSink()`, which shares the decision code with `checkTaintedSink()`; the polyglot bound-variable site keeps its mark but no longer reads it back (a same-named global assigned clean on another thread could clear it). After: 160 of 160 in 20 of 20 runs. (2) **The stdlib, file-op and polyglot rate limiters** (`RateLimiter`, unchanged since `bcf647a0`) were unlocked: TSan reported the race in 2 of 2 runs, and with a limit set concurrent increments could be lost (traced from the unlocked `++`, not measured). Now locked; TSan on the violation program: 0 warnings in 3 runs. Before A65, async fns were not rate-limited at all. Test: `tests/governance_v4/test_vm_async_governance.sh` (AG-01..05: 4 of 6 red before the engine fix, revert-checked by removing the one `setGovernance` line; AG-06, the concurrent taint count with main{} and one-async-fn controls, red with the VM taint change reverted -- 158 159 160 159 159 -- and green with it). **Traced, not reproduced, open:** `enforce()`'s escalation branch writes `check_results_.back()` under a SECOND acquisition of `results_mutex_`, so under concurrency it can mark another thread's record as escalated (the block itself still happens); and the assignment check (`OP_GOV_TAINT_CHECK_ASSIGN`) still looks the variable name up in the shared set -- it fires only when `variable.assign` is a configured sink, which no config in the tree does. **The two open items, Bundle 3 (2026-10-27):** (1) `OP_GOV_TAINT_CHECK_ASSIGN` now passes the value's own taint to `checkTaintedValueAtSink()`. The name lookup was worse than racy: the VM writes a name into the shared set only for a global, and only after this check, so a LOCAL's tainted assignment never met a `variable.assign` sink (observed: TA-01/TA-02 red on the pre-fix binary, `tests/governance_v4/test_taint_assign_sink.sh`). (2) Each CheckResult carries the `record_id` of the `enforce()` call that pushed it, and the escalation branch marks that record, not `back()`. Not reproduced: a two-rule concurrent probe (8 async fns) attributed the escalation correctly in 20 of 20 runs with the fix reverted, so this stays traced. The same probe found A86. |
| A66 | **A `for` loop over a range larger than `limits.array_size` blocks on the VM and runs on the tree-walker.** Found 2026-10-22 checking D10. The VM compiles `a..b` to `OP_RANGE`, which builds the whole list (and checks the limit, since C10), and `for` then iterates that list. The tree-walker's `a..b` is a lazy range object that is never an array, so the limit never sees it. **Observed** (`f7c3bcf1`, and again on the pre-D10 build at `b87b493`, `array_size: 1000`): `for i in 0..5000 { ... }` exits 3 on the VM ("exceeds limit of 1000") and runs all 5000 iterations on the tree-walker; `0..500` runs on both. Not introduced by D10: its `OP_RANGE` change calls the same check through the helper (traced from the diff; the pre-D10 build already blocks). | **observed, open** | Two readings, and they disagree on which engine is wrong. The limit caps memory, the VM really allocates the list, so it is right to refuse it, and the tree-walker never allocates. Or a loop is iteration, not array growth, so the VM refuses a program for an allocation it chose to make. The second reading points to iterating a range lazily on the VM (no list for a `for` over `a..b`), which removes both the false block and the allocation. That is a compiler change, not a governance one. Decide before a differential corpus entry pins either behaviour. |
| A67 | **The pipeline operator cannot feed a standard-library function.** Found 2026-10-24 in the README review: the README's own example, `"hello world" \|> string.upper() \|> string.split(" ")`, fails on both engines (`split() takes exactly 2 arguments`; `"hello" \|> string.upper()` fails with `upper() takes exactly 1 argument`). Into a user function it works on both: `"ab" \|> twice`, `\|> twice()` and `\|> shout("!")` all give the expected value (observed, `2658be11`). So the left value is not inserted as the first argument of a module call. | **observed, open** | The README now pipes into user functions and says stdlib targets do not work yet. Fixing it means deciding where the value goes in a module call (first argument, as for user functions, is the obvious reading) and doing it in both the compiler and the tree-walker; a differential probe should pin it. |
| A68 | **A risk budget that overshoots zero reads as unlimited.** Traced 2026-10-24 while checking the README's risk-budget claim, not reproduced. `consumeRiskBudget()` (`governance_engine.cpp`) subtracts without a floor, `getRemainingBudget()` returns `risk_budget - consumed`, `-1` is also its "no budget configured" sentinel, and `checkAdmission()` refuses only on `remaining == 0`. A BSD match and a drift-signal firing each cost 1, and both can land between two admission checks (one turn), so a budget of 1 can go from 1 to -1: the next send is admitted, and every later one too (any value below 0 fails `== 0`). `agent.environment()` would report the -1 as unlimited. The budget is also checked only when `exposure_tracking.enabled` is true, which the README now says. | **fixed (Bundle 3)** | Falsifier: with `risk_budget: 1`, a turn on which a user BSD pattern matches AND a CDD signal fires (S21 on a repeated reply is the cheapest), the next send should be refused and is predicted to be admitted. A fix would keep the sentinel out of the arithmetic (refuse on `remaining <= 0` when a budget is configured); it only ever turns PASS into BLOCK. **Fixed (Bundle 3, 2026-10-27):** `getRemainingBudget()` and `consumeRiskBudget()` floor at 0 when a budget is configured, and the dashboard's inline copy too, so -1 means only "no budget". The falsifier above held (observed): with `risk_budget: 1`, an advisory BSD match on `agent.response` plus an S23 coherence loss charged 2 on the first send, `risk_budget_remaining` read -1 and the next send was admitted. Two BSD patterns cannot charge twice on one event -- `recordEvent()` returns on the first pattern that completes (traced, then observed). Test: `tests/governance_v4/test_risk_budget_overshoot.sh` (6 arms; RB-00/03/04 controls; 2 mutants). |

Observed: `let d1 = {"x": 10}; d1.merge({"y": 20}); print(d1)` prints `{"x": 10}` on the VM and `{"y": 20, "x": 10}` on the tree-walker; both return the merged dict. The test file needs to fail when its count is short, or it will keep hiding the next one. |

(f) A PHP block's stdout reaches the program's own stdout as well as becoming the block's value (`<<php` `echo "x";` prints `x` and then `[x]` for `print("[" + v + "]")`, both engines); polyglot output is meant to be captured. It needs its own rewrite. Done 2026-10-25: the guide was rewritten with every program complete, each run on both engines in a fresh `naab-lang init` project by a local checker (not yet in the claims suite); the rewrite found A71-A76. | **observed, open** | (a) is the cheapest and most visible: CI recipes written flag-first fail. | A71 | **The VM ignored default parameter values.** Found 2026-10-25 by the USER_GUIDE rewrite, observed at `6f8bf002`: `fn a(name = "World") { return name }` returns `null` from `a()`; `fn c(x, y = 10)` gives `c(1)` = 2 (the tree-walker: 11); `fn d(n = 5) { let t = 100; return n }` returns 100 from `d()` -- the parameter's slot holds whatever the stack held, here the next local. The tree-walker applies every default. Traced: `Compiler` counts only the parameters without a default into `arity` (`src/vm/compiler.cpp`, "Count required params") and makes every parameter a local, but never compiles `default_value`; `CompiledFunction::default_chunks` (`include/naab/vm.h`) is copied by `deepCopyFunctionTree()` and written by nothing. Never built in the VM: the counting is in its first commit (`1dd08e83`), and the pickaxe finds no commit that fills `default_chunks`. Not in the differential corpus. A wrong value, not an error: a program gets a different answer on the default engine. | **fixed** | 2026-10-25: `callFunction()` pads the missing arguments' slots and records the call's `argc`; the compiler emits a prologue per defaulted parameter (`OP_ARG_PASSED`), evaluated in order in the callee's scope and copied unless `ref`, as on the tree-walker; `arity` is now the position of the last required parameter, so `fn f(a = 1, b)` called as `f(5)` is refused on both engines. Test: `tests/vm/test_default_params.sh` (16 arms, five mutants each turn an arm red), differential probe `default_params.naab`. |

Scalars compare by value (`5 == 5.0` is `true`). | **fixed** | Decided 2026-10-25 under the owner's "whatever is logical and correct, without loosening governance": value equality, which fits NAAb's copy-on-assign semantics; the two programs in the tree that compare a container expected it. One helper for both engines and both operators, `naab::interpreter::valuesEqual()` (`include/naab/value_equality.h`): scalars as before, lists in order, dictionaries by key, structs by type and fields. Test: `tests/vm/test_value_equality.sh`, differential probe `value_equality.naab`; the fuzz oracle models list equality instead of skipping it. |

FS_WRITE capability required` (exit 1) while `file.write("data/out.txt", ...)` succeeds; `file.exists("missing.txt")` is refused rather than `false`, `file.exists("data/missing.txt")` is `false`. `elevated` accepts all four. Traced: `Sandbox::normalizePath()` (`src/runtime/sandbox.cpp`) falls back to `weakly_canonical()`, which returns a relative name unchanged when its first component does not exist, so `isPathAllowed()` compares `out.txt` against an absolute allowed path and refuses. The same shape `checkPathAccessImpl()` had and lost (CLAUDE.md, "A relative path target is resolved against the process cwd BEFORE canonicalizing"; `tests/security/test_relative_new_path.sh` covers the governance side only). Fails closed. | **fixed** | 2026-10-25: `normalizePath()` makes a relative path absolute against the working directory before `weakly_canonical()`. Test: `tests/security/test_sandbox_relative_path.sh` (both engines; the child gets its own TMPDIR so only the working directory is allowed, and `../` and an absolute path outside stay refused). Note the CLI's `standard` is `createEnterpriseConfig()` (working directory and temp), not `fromPermissionLevel(STANDARD)` (temp and home). |
| A74 | **Run time limits: a `govern.json` limit below 30 s never applies, and the engines disagree on `--timeout`.** Observed 2026-10-25 at `6f8bf002` with a `while true` loop: with `limits.timeout.global: 10` and no flag, both engines stop at 30 s; with `--timeout 2` the VM stops at 10 s and the tree-walker at 2 s. A new project sets 60, so `--timeout` below 60 does nothing on the VM. Traced: `main.cpp` starts from the flag or 30, then `if (rules.runtime.timeout != 30) timeout = std::max(timeout, rules.runtime.timeout)`; `6c9426af` made govern.json authoritative so that "--timeout can extend but not shrink" it, and the `max` with the default 30 also means a config below 30 is raised to 30. Separately, `time.sleep()` is not interrupted: one `time.sleep(8)` under `--timeout 2` runs 8 s, then the VM raises the timeout and the tree-walker finishes, exit 0. `--help` still says "Execution timeout per block (default: 30)". | **fixed except `time.sleep()`** | 2026-10-25: with no `--timeout` the configured value applies as written (a value of 0 is ignored rather than read as "no limit"), and `--help` now describes a run limit that `--timeout` can raise but not lower; test `tests/cli/test_timeout_config.sh`. Left as decided by `6c9426af`: a stricter `--timeout` does not shrink the configured limit. Found by the fix: the `extends` merge set only `limits.timeout.global`, never `runtime.timeout`, the copy the run reads, so an inherited limit never reached the run; `test_extends.sh` T2 (parent 30, `parent_wins`, child 2) passed only because the old `max(30, child)` equalled the parent's 30, and failed once the floor was gone. The merge now carries a merged limit into `runtime.timeout` capped at the built-in 30 s, so inherited stricter limits apply and no run gets longer than before (`test_timeout_config.sh` TC-05/06). Decided by the owner 2026-10-25 ("govern.json is king") and done in A79: a flag may only LOWER a configured limit (min, reversing `6c9426af`'s extend half), an inherited limit applies as written above 30 s too, and the tree-walker reads the limit. Still open: `time.sleep()` is not interrupted. |

Which is meant is a decision; neither is the sandbox's, since the path is checked before both. | **(a)(b) FIXED 2026-10-29 (Bundle 5)**; (c)(d)(e) open | (a) and (b) mislead every user; (c) is recorded for the engine-divergence record, not acted on. **(a)(b) fixed:** `stripPolyglotBlocks()` reports which lines were a block, and `multiple_blank_lines` ends a run at each (a block's own blank lines are its language's); the function-body path tells `checkPlaceholders()`/`checkSecrets()` its text starts on `line`, so they report the line the match is on -- a TODO inside a block in main{} included, which master reported at main's line. (e) The tree-walker's expression-path taint check (`checkExpressionTaintedSink()`) ends the run under audit too, where a record and a continued run are expected (observed, `test_taint_array_transform.sh` T5); the named-variable path records. | **observed, not acted on** | (a)/(b): decide whether an audit record should carry the guidance and the detail lines; (c): trace when the tree-walker's contracts run relative to `writeReports()`; (d): a load warning naming the accepted modes; (e): route the expression path through `enforce()`. |
| A85 | **The embedded-Python audit policy read thread-local state, so a `threading.Thread` a `<<python>>` block spawns bypassed path policy and the exec/socket capability.** Found 2026-10-27 while fixing the thread leg of the A34/A35 enumeration. The hook is installed process-wide (`PySys_AddAuditHook`, `python_c_wrapper.c`) and fires on every thread that runs Python, but `naabPythonAuditPolicy` (`python_c_executor.cpp`) read `ScopedSandbox::getCurrent()` and `GovernanceEngine::getCurrent()`, both `thread_local`. A thread the block spawns runs on an OS thread where neither was installed, so the policy saw `sandbox==nullptr` / `gov==nullptr` and failed OPEN. Measured on the pre-fix binary (observed, enforce + standard/elevated, both engines' default VM): a spawned thread read a `blocked_paths` file (returned `VAULT_CONTENT`) and `os.remove`d it, and ran `os.system` under `shell.enabled:false` (created its marker), while the block's OWN thread was refused on every one (control); a grandchild thread (thread spawns a thread) too. The socket leg: with A35 (#344) the host lists and `sendto`/`sendmsg` are judged, so a spawned thread bypassed them too -- observed when master was merged in, with the fallback left out of `naabPythonNetPolicy`: a spawned thread connected to an address `allowed_hosts` does not admit, and sent a UDP datagram with `network.enabled: false` through `__import__`. Both policies now take their sandbox from one helper, `auditSandbox()`. The import blocklist (`_naab_safe_import`, a `builtins` monkeypatch) is process-wide and was never thread-local-bypassed. | **verified, fixed** | The policy falls back to the RUN-level policy when the thread-local is absent: the sandbox config the run established (`SandboxManager` default -- exactly what `vm.cpp` builds every polyglot `ScopedSandbox` from, so the fallback matches the block's own policy and adds no false denial), and a process-wide engine pointer (`GovernanceEngine::getProcessCurrent()`, published by `setCurrent`, cleared in `~GovernanceEngine` so it never dangles). It only turns PASS->BLOCK on spawned threads and only when a policy is configured (an ungoverned run is unchanged, measured). Subprocess languages stay outside path/exec policy (CONTRA-013). Test: `tests/governance_v4/test_python_thread_policy.sh` (TP-00/TP-03c/TP-04c live-gate controls; TP-02 over-block and TP-05 ungoverned-run controls; revert-checked -- TP-01/03/04/06 go red on the pristine binary, all five controls stay green). |

Found while converting the suites: an empty `/govern.json` left by a mistaken scratch command made every config-free directory discover it. | **fixed** (Bundle 2; it applies A78's rule, so no new decision) | `main.cpp` refuses right after discovery, before either engine, when `discoverAndLoad()` fails for any reason but `not_found` (an integrity failure keeps its exit 3); `/check` answers 400; `naab-gov check` exits 4 and `lint` names the cause; the REPL refuses (exit 4). `governance_c_api`'s discover and MCP already failed closed (the C API's message still says "No govern.json found"). | A89 | **A long enough input crashes the governor: `std::regex` recursion over text the governed side controls (SIGSEGV, no verdict).** Observed 2026-10-30 at `824e265c` (default unoptimised build, one run per arm). libstdc++'s `std::regex` recurses once per character on unbounded repetition (CLAUDE.md, the scanner-regex gotcha), and three routes reach it with input the model or the program author chooses: (a) an operator's `output_contract.regex_checks` pattern (`^[a-z ]+$`) over a 60,000-character field of a model reply exits 139 (`agent_impl.cpp`, the regex_checks loop) -- controls: the same field with no regex, and a short field under the regex, are both delivered; (b) the engine's OWN PII scan (`code_quality.no_pii`, no operator pattern involved) over a 60,000- or 300,000-character reply exits 139, while `no_secrets` survives both (its patterns were bounded earlier) and the run with both scans off delivers; (c) the whole-file PII check over a program holding a 60,000-character string literal exits 139 before the program runs; with `no_pii` off the same program runs (exit 0). The mechanism, alone in a 10-line C++ program: `^[a-z ]+$` matches 10,000 characters and segfaults at 50,000. Reach: a provider reply is bounded by `max_tokens` (about 4 characters a token, so ~12,000+ output tokens for 50,000 characters at `-O0`; the Release threshold is unmeasured); tool results are cut to `tool_result_max_chars` before they are scanned; program text, and so route (c), is bounded by nothing. Screened, not run: 11 sites build a `std::regex` from config or program input (`governance_checks.cpp` 4995/5355, `governance_config.cpp` 2223, `governance_engine.cpp` 2124/2182, `agent_impl.cpp` 4547, `orchestra_impl.cpp` 208 -- the last takes its pattern from the PROGRAM), and the `regex` stdlib module runs `std::regex` through `SafeRegex`, whose 100 KB input cap sits above the crash size and whose timeout thread dies with the process. A crash is worse than a false negative: no verdict, no report, exit 139 rather than 3. | **fixed (Bundle 6): crash and stall; the fallback is bounded, not linear** -- residual below | **Fixed.** Every regex in the engine goes through `naab::rx` (`include/naab/rx.h`; 485 sites in 43 files; `tests/self-audit/test_no_std_regex.sh` keeps `std::regex` out). A pattern RE2 (2025-11-05, vendored) can read exactly as `std::regex` does is matched by RE2, in linear time; the rest -- lookaround, backreferences, ECMAScript-only escapes -- by QuickJS's libregexp, which backtracks on the heap (no crash). **Differential** (observed, this container, the RE2 commit `b3d377a7` built with `-DNAAB_REGEX_DIFFERENTIAL=ON`, 4 jobs): the whole suite, 361 units, every search of 4 KB or less also run on `std::regex` and compared: 0 mismatches. Two failures, neither the matcher's: RP-09/csharp (pre-existing), and `test_langconform.sh` at its 600 s bound, since the oracle doubles every search. Blind spots: inputs over 4 KB were not compared; a unit whose environment is scrubbed logs nothing. Its list of patterns that took the fallback had 125 entries: 110 lookahead, 6 backreference, 9 test or malformed. **The stall, found from that list** (observed, unoptimised build, bare enforce config): a `<<python>>` block with one 8,000-character line held the governor past 300 s with no verdict, and so did the pre-`naab::rx` `std::regex` build. gdb placed it in the dangerous-alias patterns, `(\w+)\s*=\s*\b(os\.system)\b(?!\s*\()`, tried at every position of one word. A 30,000-character comment line did the same under `no_oversimplification`, in the comment co-occurrence patterns, which tried every marker on a line. Fixed in `88ba3bc3`: (1) the alias left-hand sides start with `\b`, which changes no match (the word must end before `\s*=`); (2) the 12 co-occurrence patterns try only the first marker on a line, through a lookbehind placed after the marker, and `Rx.FirstCommentMarkerChangesNoMatch` compares the old and new forms on 18,000 seeded lines; (3) a step budget. The vendored QuickJS gets a backport of QuickJS 2024's regexp timeout (`external/patches/`, recorded in `DEPENDENCIES.lock`, checked by `tests/self-audit/test_vendored_patches.sh`). Each fallback search gets `kSearchSteps` (1e8), and `checkPolyglotBlock`/`checkNaabFunctionBody`/`checkNaabSourceText` each share one `ScanBudget` (`kScanSteps`, 4e8). A scan past it is refused at HARD (`governance.scan_budget`, exit 3), never passed unscanned, and nine `catch(...)` sites around fallback-capable regexes rethrow it. After: 8,000 and 60,000 characters in 0.2 and 0.6 s, the `#` line in 0.7 s, a 60,000-character line with 3,000 `//` in 1.4 s. **Sibling, closed by the same hook:** `--timeout 3` did not stop a `<<javascript>>` block in a catastrophic regexp (ran 120 s, until killed from outside); it now stops at 3 s. **Residual (traced):** (a) a fallback pattern that is superlinear on some input -- a built-in lookahead not yet rewritten, or an operator's custom pattern -- still costs up to the budget, about 6 s on the unoptimised build, before the refusal; (b) the agent-path scans (secrets and PII over replies, `output_contract.regex_checks`) run outside the static entry points, under the per-search budget only. Past it they raise a runtime error, not `governance.scan_budget`: the content is not delivered, but a NAAb `try/catch` can catch the error; (c) a build without QuickJS (MSVC; CI builds MinGW) falls back to `std::regex`, unbounded; (d) the regex module's cap on abandoned workers (SafeRegex, 4) is no longer reached on a QuickJS build, since an abandoned worker now ends at its step budget, so `test_regex_timeout_bound.sh` C-02 reports it UNMEASURABLE and nothing asserts it there (its group C now asserts only that six catastrophic calls all stop); (e) cost, found and fixed: RE2 compiles each pattern once per process, and the default local build (no build type) compiled RE2 itself at -O0. The compiler then dominated a governed run: 53% of all instructions in a two-block run (callgrind), and `test_langconform.sh`, which starts thousands of naab-gov checks, took 616-620 s against master's 366 s (observed, same day, same container) and timed out at its 600 s bound. `c2e3ccb4` builds the re2 target at -O2 when no build type is set. After it: one naab-gov check takes 31 ms against master's 82 ms, and `test_langconform.sh` takes 161 s against 366 s. A first re-timing at 620 s had used a naab-gov not yet relinked, a stale binary measured as the subject. Tests: `tests/security/test_long_input_regex.sh` (21 arms; LR-07..LR-10 are the stall and the JavaScript timeout), `tests/unit/rx_test.cpp` (17), `tests/self-audit/test_vendored_patches.sh`; the kill tables are in the suite headers. |
| A90 | **`DEPENDENCIES.lock` pins a sha256 for the QuickJS archive that is not the archive's.** Observed 2026-10-30: `external/quickjs-2021-03-27.tar.xz` hashes to `a45bface4c3379538dea8533878d694e289330488ea7028b105f72572fe7fe1a`; the lock (both entries) pins `a45d90d59cbeb3a6ca7a7092d9db17ef7c9f37cb0ddfef30a1e4aa0c97c44d5b`. The pin was written in `f666e6e5` (2026-02-02, "Fix Option C compilation errors"). Nothing compared the two until `tests/self-audit/test_vendored_patches.sh`, written for A89's patch, did. The unpacked archive equals the vendored tree apart from the A89 patch, so the code built is the archive's. | A91 | **`restrictions.dangerous_calls` refuses any anonymous JavaScript function as the `Function()` constructor.** `checkDangerousCall()` (`governance_engine.cpp`) compiles every `DANGEROUS_PATTERNS_DB` entry with `icase`, so `\bFunction\s*\(`, meant for the constructor, also matches the keyword `function (`. JavaScript is case-sensitive, so the two are different things. Observed 2026-10-30 with one probe: `[1, 2, 3].map(function (v) { return v * 2; })` in a `<<javascript>>` block under `"dangerous_calls": "hard"` is refused (`Matched: "function ("`) on this branch, on the pre-`naab::rx` build and on an older TSan build, so the matcher is not the cause. Found by luck, not method: a timing probe written for A89 was refused before it ran, and so measured a refusal. | **FIXED** (Bundle 7) | History: #47 (`915b43af`) found this exact false positive in `checkCodeInjection()` and made that copy's API-name patterns case-sensitive; `checkDangerousCall()` was never changed (a fix that landed in one copy). This copy was NOT made case-sensitive, because that would let through PHP's case-insensitive function names (`SYSTEM(...)` runs) and an alias spelled in another case (`FUNCTION(...)`), both refused today. (A Python `import os as OS` alias is resolved before this check, so it is not one of them.) Instead `DangerousPattern` gained `keyword`: a match spelled exactly as that reserved word is skipped and the search goes on, so only `function (` itself is exempt -- a reserved word cannot name a variable, so it is never the constructor or an alias of it. Sweep (screened): the 104 `DANGEROUS_PATTERNS_DB` entries and the 29 network and 28 filesystem import patterns checked against javascript, python, ruby, shell, go, rust, cpp and csharp keyword lists; `Function`/`function` is the only collision. PHP, Nim and shell keep case-insensitive matching on purpose (PHP function names and case-insensitive filesystems). Test: `tests/governance_v4/test_dangerous_calls_keyword.sh` (DK-01/01b red on the unfixed binary; DK-04 is the alias arm a case-sensitive fix fails, DK-05 the arm a first-match-only fix fails, DK-06 the control that the rest of the table still ignores case, PHP `SYSTEM(...)`). Revert-checked: keyword compared without case turns DK-02..DK-05 red, first match only DK-05, the whole check case-sensitive DK-04 and DK-06. |
| A92 | **The cycle collector cleared lists another engine was using, so a program computed with empty lists whenever two VMs ran at once.** The collector (V-RT-008, `4e05c814`) marks from ONE VM's stack and globals, sweeps every heap value in the process, and clears any unreachable list, dict or struct whose refcount is above 1 as a cycle. Since `4f65b2b0` every `async fn` runs its own VM with its own collector, and the per-thread handle ranges (`4dbd5988`) put every thread's values in the one table the sweep walks. A value another VM is using is never among the collector's roots, and often has a refcount above 1 for a moment -- each read pushes a stack copy, and a collection holds a copy of each of its own roots. Observed 2026-10-31 on `ca07ae12` (mode off, default build): main looping beside a running async fn read its own list as 0 elements in 6 of 6 runs (the same loop alone, 3 in 6 of 6); an async fn holding a list beside a sibling async fn, 0 in 3 of 5 (5 of 5 in an earlier run); `gc_collect()` in main while an async fn held a list, 0 in 4 of 4. Main's lists survived while main was blocked at `await`, because main was not collecting then -- lists are copied on assignment, so a refcount above 1 is transient, and only a CONCURRENT collector sees it. Disabling the async VMs' collection (an experiment, `setGCThreshold(0)` in `callValue`) read 3 in 8 of 8, which put the cause in the collector. A wider window was found while testing the fix: the worker copies its globals and pushes its arguments before its own `run()` begins, and main's collections in that window cleared an async fn's list ARGUMENT in about 96% of calls (observed with that half of the fix removed: 360, 357 and 255 of 9000). History: never built -- no commit gave the collector a concurrency rule. The tree-walker's half is not reachable: its async fns run synchronously (ASYNC-001), and its automatic collector tracked 13 allocations against a threshold of 5000 in a 60000-iteration loop, so only `gc_collect()` runs it. | **FIXED** | `detectAndCollect()` runs only when its engine is the only activity in the process (`src/interpreter/cycle_detector.h`): `VM::run()` and `Interpreter::execute()` record an `EngineActivation` per engine per thread (a nested callback on the same engine is free), the async spawn counts the new VM as a `PendingEngine` from before its thread starts until its body returns, and the gate's lock is held for the whole collection. A collection that cannot run is deferred to a later trigger, never waited for. Consequence, by design: an async or module VM never collects (its caller is always executing), and main collects only while no async fn is running, so a long-lived async fn delays cycle collection until it finishes. `--gc-stats` now reports the engine that ran (it printed the idle tree-walker's count on the VM, always 0, and called cleared values "Collections run") with collections run, deferred and values collected. Test: `tests/vm/test_gc_engine_activity.sh` (8 arms; GA-04/05/07 are controls, GA-05 that main alone still collects) and `tests/unit/gc_engine_activity_test.cpp` (8 tests, each reason to defer beside the control that the probe list is cleared once the reason is gone; the two-VM test is the only arm that catches a missing `run()` activation). Not covered: threads that are not engines -- the `agent.batch` pool builds values while main is blocked in the native call, so main cannot collect then, but nothing records them (screened, from the thread-start list); `ffi_async_callback` not traced. |
| A93 | **A tool ran on the MAIN VM from whatever thread the tool loop was on, so `agent.batch` ran two tools on one VM stack at once, and an async fn's tool ran on main's stack while main executed.** Traced 2026-10-31 while fixing A92 (the batch route); reproduced 2026-11-01 on `19cebb05` with the stub, two handles and a tool that loops 200,000 times. Observed: `max_concurrent` 2, 5 of 5 runs exit 1 -- the tools returned "Modulo requires integers" from a loop over integers and main's own batch result read null afterwards; `max_concurrent` 1, 5 of 5 correct (599994 each). The class has a second route the row did not name, found by asking which VM an async fn's agent call reaches: main.cpp bound the tool callback to `bytecode_vm` at startup, and since A65 the async VM shares the engine, so an `agent.send` inside an async fn ran its tool on MAIN's stack while main was still executing. Observed (5 runs, an async fn sending while main ran its own loop): SIGSEGV twice, a wrong sum in main once, main's `file` module read as null, "Dangling handle" in the tool, and the async fn's tool never recorded its call. History: never built -- `agent.batch` (`b9f0774b`, 2026-05-06) predates tool execution (`2d3a9d64`, 2026-06-03), which bound the callback to the main VM and names no threads; no doc decides otherwise. Thread starts enumerated with the code map (`codemap threads`, screened, each read): `agentBatch` is the only one that runs NAAb code on a VM it does not own (async fns build their own VM, the FFI async pool has test callers only, the MCP server starts processes). | **FIXED** | A tool runs on the VM the agent call came from (`include/naab/thread_vm.h`): `VM::run()` records the VM executing on its thread (`RunningVMScope`), and main.cpp's callback is `VM::callToolOnThreadVM()`, a nested call on that VM; `agentBatch` lends its own VM to the workers (`VMLoan`, `LentVMScope`), which enter it one at a time under the loan's lock while the dispatcher waits in `agent.batch`. A loan is revoked when the dispatcher leaves by any path (a worker's HARD block), so a worker still in its tool loop is refused instead of entering a VM that is unwinding; a worker already inside finishes that call and is not waited for, so a long tool cannot turn a HARD block into a hang. The decision between a lock and a VM per tool, from the result: a fresh VM per tool drops the dispatcher's frames, and function-scope capabilities intersect down the call stack, so it would LOOSEN them (mutant M4 turns TV-03 red); the lent VM keeps them. Consequence: batched tools run one at a time (the model calls stay concurrent), and an async fn's tool now runs on the async fn's VM, attributed to its frames. Test: `tests/governance_v4/test_tool_thread_vm.sh` (8 arms; TV-00b proves the two sends overlap, TV-03/TV-04 attribution with controls; red on `fc9b8099`: TV-01, TV-02, TV-04) and `tests/unit/thread_vm_test.cpp` (`ThreadVm.*`: revocation, scope restore, four workers on one lent VM). |
| A94 | **Under enforce, a misspelled `security.sandbox_level` switched off the clamp to `standard`, so the run kept whatever level it had: `unrestricted` with no flag, `elevated` with `--sandbox-level elevated`.** The clamp tested `sandbox_level_config.empty()` -- "govern.json set no level" -- and a misspelling ("bogus", "elevaetd") is not empty, while it applies nothing either (#241, A13, decided to warn about it rather than reject it: "No sandbox level was applied from govern.json"). So an enforce project with a typo ran with the CLI default, which is unrestricted. History: never built for an invalid value -- the condition dates from the fail-closed boundary work (`52b880e5`, `56f25210`). Found by `tests/property/ratchet_property.cpp`'s exhaustive check of the CLI overlays: "under enforce, flag elevated with no configured level gives elevated, above standard" (2026-10-31, authored check over the functions main.cpp calls). | **FIXED** | The decisions moved to one header, `include/naab/run_limits.h` (`configuredLevelApplies()`, `enforceClampApplies()`, `effectiveSandboxLevel()`, `applyConfiguredRunLimits()`), called by both of main.cpp's run paths, which held two copies. Only a real level grants a level above `standard` under enforce, and a misspelled one no longer becomes the run's level name while rebuilding nothing; it still only warns (#241's decision kept). Test: `test_ratchet_property.sh` A94 (`env.get` refused by the standard sandbox under `"bogus"`; controls: `"elevated"` reads, no level is clamped) and RP-01's flag-overlay check (348 combinations). |
| A95 | **An environment's `mode` changed the rules but not whether governance was active, and three things followed.** `loadFromFile()` sets the engine's active flag from the FILE's mode; `applyEnvironment()` (`--env`, `governance.default_env`) then rewrote `rules().mode` and left the flag alone. Observed 2026-10-31 (CLI, VM, elevated sandbox, a `blocked_read` variable): (a) **fail-open** -- a file in mode off with `--env x` (`x: {mode: enforce}`) read the variable, exit 0: the gates whose callers check `isActive()` never ran; (b) an environment with `mode: off` over an enforce or audit file left the engine active with rules saying off, and `enforce()` exempts only AUDIT, so the run BLOCKED, exit 3 -- stricter than the file, even where the file said audit; (c) the first accepted reload recomputed the flag from the overlaid mode, so under (b) it switched every gate off -- and the ratchet could not see it, because it compares the rules' modes (off -> off). Two more routes into (c) by the same blindness: the file going enforce -> off under such an environment (compared off -> off), and the environment going off -> audit (compared as a tightening, after which nothing blocked). All three (c) routes were found by `tests/property/ratchet_property.cpp` (seeds 1, 6 and 9 at 300-400 cases). History: never built -- environments predate the A37 overlay replay, and nothing gave them a rule for activity. | **(a), (c) FIXED; (b) open -- the owner's decision** | (a): `applyEnvironment()` raises the flag when the environment's mode is audit or enforce, and never lowers it. (c): a reload computes the flag as startup does (the file's own mode, or an overlay that raises it), and `evaluateReload()` refuses a reload whose ENFORCED mode is weaker -- an active engine whose rules say off counts as enforcing, which is what `enforce()` does with it today -- naming "mode: enforced enforce -> off/audit". Test: `test_ratchet_property.sh` A95a (control: the same file without `--env` reads the variable) and A95b (control: a tightening under that environment is accepted). (b) is left as it is: whether an environment's `mode: off` should switch governance off is a decision, and doing it would loosen today's behaviour (a run that blocks would stop blocking). |
| A96 | **Two reloads the ratchet accepted loosened governance.** (a) With `capabilities.env_vars.read: false` the ratchet skipped the read lists ("with the switch off on either side the lists decide nothing", #303), but `blocked_read` is also withheld from every child process the program starts (enforce mode), whatever the switch says -- so a reload could drop a name from `blocked_read` and every later child received it. (b) Levels were compared by `levelToInt()` (advisory < detect < soft < approval < hard), but under `--governance-override` a SOFT block lets the action through while DETECT still refuses it, so detect -> soft was accepted and stopped refusing in every run with the override on. Both found by `tests/property/ratchet_property.cpp` (2026-10-31): (a) `env.child:AWS_SECRET before=blocked after=allowed`, (b) `scan.pii before=blocked(catchable) after=allowed` with the override. History: (a) #303's premise (2026-10-11) was wrong when written -- `blocked_read` had been withheld from children since #302, the day before; (b) never built -- the override was not part of the order. | **FIXED** | (a): with either switch off the ratchet compares `blocked_read` as the child-process list ("child-process read lists"); with both on the read comparison already covers it, since only `blocked_read` yields Blocked. (b): `levelLoosens()` -- a change loosens when the new level refuses less in a run with the override or without it (`levelRefusal()`), so detect -> soft is refused ("a soft block can be overridden, detect cannot") and every other step keeps its old verdict. Test: `test_ratchet_property.sh` A96a and A96b (controls: adding a name, detect -> hard, are accepted). |
| A97 | **A capability lease widened what child processes received.** A lease without ENV_READ forces `subprocess_scrub_mode: "allowlist"` (`applyLeaseGrantToRules()`, #307), and allowlist mode dropped the project's `blocked_subprocess_vars` / `blocked_subprocess_prefixes` while it always passes the essential names (PATH, HOME, LANG, ...). So a project that withheld HOME or PATH from children passed them under a lease. Found by `tests/property/ratchet_property.cpp`'s overlay property (a lease must narrow what it is laid over): `env.child:PATH before=blocked after=allowed`, seeds 1-5 at 400 cases. Low severity -- the names allowlist mode passes are not secrets -- but the lease is meant only to narrow. | **FIXED** | A blocked name or prefix is scrubbed in either mode (`envScrubListsFrom()` keeps the blocked lists under an allowlist, `subprocessListsScrub()` checks them first), and embedded Python's `os.environ` scrub, which generates the same rule as Python code, was changed with it. Only ever scrubs more: no config in the tree combines an allowlist with blocked lists. Test: `test_ratchet_property.sh` A97 (`process.run("printenv", ["HOME"])` under `--lease-grant SHELL_EXEC`; controls: withheld without the lease, passed under it when nothing blocks it). |
| A98 | **An `extends` child can loosen its parent under both merge strategies, `parent_wins` included; only the mode floor holds.** Measured 2026-10-31 by `naab_ratchet_property --cases 0 --extends 2000 --seed 3` (1,918 of 2,000 generated parent/child pairs loaded; observed), re-measured after the generator wrote roles in the form the loader reads (A100) -- an `extends` pair starts with no role, so that fix changed only which pairs load: every strategy and array mode had pairs where the child allowed a request the parent refused, among them environment reads, writes and child-process withholding, file reads and writes, languages and the response scans, and in three of the four modes hosts -- under `parent_wins`/`append` a child's allowlist entries widen the parent's (an `allowed_hosts` entry, an `allowed_read` name); under `parent_wins`/`replace` a child's `blocked_read`, `languages.blocked` or `blocked_subprocess_vars` replaces the parent's (`mergeStringVec()` keeps a non-empty child list whatever the strategy); and a child's `no_pii: false` or `no_secrets` level overrides the parent's. No pair lowered the mode below the parent's ("Mode is a security floor", `mergeRules()`). `child_wins` letting a child override is the documented design; `parent_wins` reads "base value takes precedence", which the arrays and several scalars do not do. | **open -- the owner's decision** | What should `extends` promise? Settled by the owner. If `parent_wins` is to mean the parent's policy holds, the merge must take the stricter of the two for every security setting (blocked lists unioned, allowlists intersected with care -- two disjoint allowlists must not intersect to an empty list, which reads as unrestricted -- switches ANDed, levels by `levelRefusal()`), and the harness would then assert it. Until then the harness reports the table and asserts the mode floor only. |

Traced 2026-10-31 while changing that code for A97, not measured: under an allowlist, `os.environ` in a `<<python>>` block keeps the parent's LD_LIBRARY_PATH, and a process the block starts itself inherits it. | **open** | Not changed with A97 because removing it could break Python's own subprocesses where the loader needs it (Termux), which cannot be tested here. Settle by measuring on Termux, then generating the Python list from `essentialChildEnvVars()`. |
| A100 | **An `agents` (or `agent_roles`) block written as a list was ignored without a word, so roles written that way restricted nothing.** The loader reads the block only when it is an object keyed by role name (`j[agents_key].is_object()`, since `50c793a5`); a list of `{"name": ...}` objects loaded as NO agents, so `--agent-id` matched nothing and every per-agent narrowing (paths, languages, shell, network, the action matrix) was absent. Found 2026-10-31 by the ratchet property harness itself: its generator wrote roles as a list, so every "role" case was in fact role-less and the role property passed vacuously -- the harness's controls did not notice, because each probe still varied with the other settings. No config in the tree uses the list form (checked: every tracked JSON with a top-level `agents`/`agent_roles`, 80 in object form, 0 in list form). | **FIXED (warning)** | The loader now warns when the block is present and not an object: `"agents" must be an object keyed by agent name ...; it was ignored, so no agent role applies`. Refusing the config instead (exit 4) would be the fail-closed choice; left as a warning because nothing in the tree is affected either way and the precedent for a malformed section is a load warning -- the owner's call if it should refuse. The harness now writes the object form and fails its run when a role or a lease never changes a verdict (overlay liveness controls). Test: `test_ratchet_property.sh` A100 (control: the object form applies the role and prints no warning). |
| A101 | **Removing a role's last ENV action loosened the role, and the ratchet read it as a tightening.** ENV_READ / ENV_WRITE are opt-in: a role's `allowed_actions` governs environment access only when it names at least one of them (`checkEnvVarRead()`/`checkEnvVarWrite()`, since `4589acd3`). Removing any other action is unchanged. Test: `test_ratchet_property.sh` A101 (control: removing FS_READ, env still governed, is accepted). |

| A110 | **A provider's reported token counts could un-spend the run's token budget, and a count past `int`'s range wrapped.** The budgets (`agent_dispatch.hard_stop.max_tokens_per_run`, per-agent `max_total_tokens`) add the counts in the response body (`usageMetadata.promptTokenCount`/`candidatesTokenCount`/`thoughtsTokenCount`, Anthropic `usage.input_tokens`/`output_tokens`), read with `get<int>()` and taken as given. Observed on master `fc9b8099` (stub, a 1,000-token run budget, the first reply 900 tokens): a second reply reporting -500,000 input and -500,000 output tokens drove the run total to -999,100 and all four sends went out (TC-01); one reporting 2^62 and 2^32-1 read as 0 and -1 and three went out (TC-02). History: never built -- the parse sites predate the budgets that sum them. | **FIXED**; residual open (traced) | Every count is read as 64-bit and clamped to [0, 10,000,000] where it is parsed (`reportedTokenCount()`, `agent_provider.cpp`, all five sites), so a reply can only add, and adds at most 10M per count. `tests/governance_v4/test_reported_token_counts.sh` (TC-00 the control; kill table in its header, observed). **Residual**: the totals are still `int` (`std::atomic<int>` run total, `int` trackers -- `NaabVal::makeInt` takes `int`), so with every count at the cap a total wraps after about 2.1 billion tokens; a budget below `INT_MAX` minus one reply's maximum charge (20M: input plus output) stops the run before that, so only a budget within 20M of 2,147,483,647 could still be crossed by a wrap (traced, not reproduced). |

History: never built -- the parsers were written lenient and nothing reported a dropped value. | **FIXED** for `capabilities`, `languages`, `agents`; remainder open | `checkRestrictionTypes()` at the top of `loadFromJson()` (every load and reload path) refuses such a config, exit 4, naming the key and the type it must be; a mid-run reload to one is a config error (`naab-gov ratchet` exit 4). | A112 | **The agent stub logged a request AFTER sending its reply, so its key log could be out of order or short.** `agent_stub.py` wrote each `keys.log` line after `wfile.write()`: a sequential client could send request 2, and have it logged, before request 1's line was written, and a stub stopped after the last reply (SIGTERM) lost a line not yet written. Every suite that reads the log by line (`test_concurrent_key_rotation.sh` CK-11 takes line 1 as the first request; CK-09 counts lines) could then fail with the engine behaving correctly. Found 2026-11-01 by the schedule-perturbation runner on its first run: under one CPU, CK-11 failed in 1 of 3 rounds, reading request 2's key (`bravo`) on line 1 while the throttle named request 1's key (`alpha`), as in all 30 one-CPU replays of the arm (observed: in each, request 1 used `alpha` and the throttle named it). A delay planted between the reply and the old log call lost request 1's line in 3 of 3 runs (observed). Test-harness defect, not engine behaviour. History: never built -- the log line was added after the reply for its end time (`t1`). | **FIXED** | The line is written before the body, after `hold_ms`, so `t1` still bounds the window the overlap probes compare; a client cannot see reply N before line N exists. `test_stub_routing.sh` SR-08 holds the logging step with the new `log_hold_ms` fixture key and requires request 1 first and both lines present after the stop (red on the old order, D7 in its header; SR-08c the control). **A second loss, Windows only**: two handler threads appending at once lost a line, because a Windows append is not atomic between file handles (the C runtime seeks to the end, then writes). Observed on build-windows (#354): `test_tool_thread_vm.sh` TV-00b's printed windows held three of four requests in each of 3 runs, one of the two concurrent first calls missing although both had been answered. POSIX `O_APPEND` is atomic, so no Linux run shows it. Every append to `keys.log`, `routes.log` and `pairing.log` now goes through one locked helper (`append_log()`); SR-09 checks from the stub's syntax tree that every append-mode `open()` is under that lock (D8 removes it; SR-09c is the planted control). |
| A113 | **A completed behavioural sequence could go unenforced four ways, each a pattern the operator wrote to BLOCK that did not** (`src/runtime/behavioral_sequence.cpp`, Bundle 10). (2)+(3) `wouldMatch()` (the pre-execution check) skipped any pattern at step 0, so a SINGLE-STEP pattern was never checked before the call -- a `no file.write` HARD pattern let the write land, then blocked after (exit 3 with the file on disk). (4) a partial match kept its OLDEST start, so reading a credential, running five ordinary calls, reading it again and sending never fired `credential_harvesting` although a fresh read sat one event before the send (exit 0). History: the single-cursor matcher is original (`e928c1a7`); none of the four was ever a decision. | **FIXED** (A113) | The matcher keeps a BOUNDED LIST of partial matches per pattern and runs ONE advance function (`advancePattern()`) that `recordEvent` commits and `wouldMatch` simulates on a copy, so the pre-check cannot predict one thing and the commit do another. Every pattern is advanced on each event and the STRICTEST completion is enforced (fix 1); a fresh partial starts on any step-0 match that did not complete the pattern, so a stalled anchor is superseded (fix 4) and single-step patterns fire -- the pre-check now catches them (fix 2/3). Matching stays NON-OVERLAPPING (the `test_bsd_preexec_double_record.sh` oracle still counts 3, not 5). The shared function returns the FULL completed sequence, blocked step included -- the completed path's convention -- so `checkPreExecution()` no longer adds one to the count or appends the blocked detail again: before that last step the full suite caught this branch reporting the README quickstart's 3-step block as "4-step" (`test_readme_claims.sh` RC-02b), now pinned directly by BE-04. Four revert-checked mutants (strictest->first; pre-check skips stateless; dedup keeps oldest; the pre-check's old +1), controls green; the existing BSD suites, the BSD unit tests and the MCP cross-call sequence suite (MC-17/MC-19) still pass. **Load-time lint** (B10.1, `governance_config.cpp`): a pattern with a step that has no matcher is reported as never-firing, and a pattern subsumed by another (identical steps, an equal-or-wider window at an equal-or-stricter level) is reported redundant -- report-only, never refuses, sound by construction (identical step sequences only, so no firing pattern is called redundant); `tests/governance_v4/test_bsd_pattern_lint.sh` (PL-02c/PL-02d the soundness controls). **State-machine explorer** (B10.2, `tools/governance_explore/explore.py`, report-only): bounded exploration drives the real engine over every ordering of propose/commit/sibling-send/proposer-send and checks that no commit lands at CRITICAL (INV1) and the level never de-escalates (INV2); depth 4 = 173 sequences, clean (observed), with a successful-commit positive control and a CRITICAL-reached guard so a pass is not vacuous. `tests/governance_v4/test_governance_statemachine.sh` is the depth-3 CI smoke (SM-02 the negative control: a planted violation makes the tool exit 1). |
| A114 | **The VM checked a function's output contract only on an explicit `return value`.** `checkFunctionContract()` ran at `OP_RETURN`; `OP_RETURN_NULL` -- a bare `return`, or a body that falls off its end without a value -- ran nothing, so a function with `return_not_null: true` (or `return_type`, `return_keys`, ...) returned null under `Governance: PASS`. Observed on master `f9337bdf`: `fn h(x) { if x > 0 { return } return 1 }` with `return_not_null`, `h(1)` returned null, exit 0, while `return null` was refused, exit 3. History: `f6e5f3b8` (2026-03-28) added the VM's contract check to `OP_RETURN` alone; nothing records a decision to skip the null return. Found by the Bundle 11 design trace (every VM return path, for `ensures`). Under enforce every program runs on the VM, so this held on every enforced run. | **FIXED** (Bundle 11) | Both return opcodes now go through `VM::checkReturnContracts()`, which runs the `ensures` clauses and the output contract. At `OP_RETURN_NULL` it runs only for a function that has a contract, so a null return of any other function sets no check location (the unscoped `setCheckContext()` inside `checkFunctionContract()` would stamp it on the next check). `tests/governance_v4/test_contract_clauses.sh` NR-01 (red on master) beside NR-01c (a value return still passes), EN-03 (`ensures` on a bare return); revert-checked (mutant M4: `OP_RETURN_NULL` skips the checks -> NR-01, EN-03). A tightening: a program whose contracted function returned null this way, and passed, is now refused. |
| A115 | **A lease or a capability manifest that grants FS_WRITE without FS_READ switches the filesystem off.** The filesystem mode has no write-only setting, so the narrowing the lease and the manifest share (`narrowCapabilitiesToGrant()`, `governance_config.cpp`) sets the mode to `none` whenever FS_READ is withheld, and writes are refused with the reads. Observed with the manifest: the first suite run failed MF-07 (a program that only writes, drafted with FS_WRITE alone, could not write) until `naab-gov manifest infer` listed FS_READ beside FS_WRITE -- the manifest's `files.read` list still bounds what is read. Traced for the MCP lease: `naab_lease` granting `[FS_WRITE]` refuses every write. Fail-closed. | **open** (decision) | Whether a write-only mode should exist. One would let a grant of FS_WRITE alone write where today it can do nothing -- a loosening of the current `none`, though narrower than also granting FS_READ -- so it needs every read route closed under it (the stdlib readers, embedded Python's `open()`, the sandbox's FS_READ) before it is a decision to take. |
| A116 | **A lease without FS_READ refuses the project's governance plugins.** A plugin file is read through the module loader's gate (A25: `checkFilesystemAllowed("read")`, then `checkPathAccess()`), and `checkFilesystemAllowed()` asks `checkFunctionCapability("FS_READ")`, where the lease is a HARD set (`api.lease`); `loadPlugins()` rethrows a `GovernanceHardError`, so the run is refused, not the plugin skipped. Traced for the MCP lease, not run. The capability manifest exempts plugin files from its files layer and its module pin (the operator's governance code, not the program's), but its FS_READ action binds the read the same way, so `infer` adds FS_READ when govern.json lists plugins (observed: test_capability_manifest.sh MF-22c). | **open** (decision) | Whether the operator's plugin loading should answer to the caller's action grant at all. The gate cannot tell a plugin read from a program import today; exempting it needs that distinction (the tree-walker's `plugin_loading_depth_` is the one marker), and must not admit a program import. |

| # | claim (as reported) | state | evidence |

| F31 | tamper-evident log: empty signature bypasses HMAC verification | **VERIFIED + FIXED** | #218. Worse than reported — see below |
| F32 | hardcoded Termux TLS bundle used as the fallback for every platform | **VERIFIED (trace)** | live on `3644c67`; `agent_provider.cpp:117`, `telemetry_forwarder.cpp:155`. Written up as a CLAUDE.md gotcha, not yet fixed |
| F33 | `use module` bypasses filesystem governance | **VERIFIED + FIXED (engine-parity, not a flat bypass)** | Re-probed on `c5a645f`. It is a VM/tree-walk SPLIT: `use secret` under `filesystem.mode:none` is BLOCKED on the VM (exit 3) and BYPASSES on `--tree-walk` (`USED=USE_SECRET`, PASS, exit 0). Cause is a NAMESAKE — two classes expose `parseModuleFile`: `ModuleResolver` (`module_resolver.cpp`, gated by A25/#213, which the VM reaches) and `ModuleRegistry` (`module_system.cpp:82`, ungated, which the tree-walker reaches via `modules.cpp:247` -> `loadModule`). **A25 was therefore half a fix** — its own commit message warned it had to land on both engines or it would reproduce the B11-class divergence, and it did not. The reporter's trace named the ungated class correctly but implied it was the universal `use` path; and an earlier note here inferred "still open" from a grep for gates in `module_system.cpp` — a MENTION-level trace that happened to reach the right answer for the wrong reason. Fixed by gating `ModuleRegistry::parseModuleFile` with the same `checkFilesystemAllowed` + `checkPathAccess` pair. Test: `tests/governance_v4/test_use_module_governance.sh` 4/4 — UM-01 (VM) is the control that the gate exists and passed pre-fix, UM-02 (`--tree-walk`) is the fix and failed pre-fix, UM-03 the no-over-block control on the changed engine. |
| F34 | native `io` stdlib ignores `blocked_paths` | **VERIFIED + FIXED** | reproduced on `fb1e4bd`, both engines: `file.read` exit 3, `io.read_file` exit 0 on the same blocked path. Also `write_file`/`exists`/`list_dir`, and `io.write_file` OVERWROTE `govern.json` at `Governance: PASS`. `io`+`env` added to `filesystemAccessMode()`. Test: `test_io_env_fs_gate.sh` |
| F35 | **`debug.is_tainted` answers `false` for tainted data under the VM** | **VERIFIED (positive control), fix open — an accessor re-route is NOT the fix, see below** | Measured on `bf489f0`, `taint_tracking.sources: ["file.read"]`: `let v = file.read(...)` reports `true` on `--tree-walk` and `false` on the VM, at BOTH main scope and function scope; an untainted literal reports `false` on both (negative control). Unsafe direction and silent — a script guarding `if debug.is_tainted(x) { refuse }` never refuses on the default engine. NOT itself a governance gate: taint SINKS are enforced separately on both engines and were never affected. Pinned by `tests/governance_v4/test_debug_is_tainted_parity.sh` (DT-03/DT-04 assert the defect is still present, so fixing it turns the suite red and forces this row to be updated). |
| F36 | **agent tool execution is VM-only; under `--tree-walk` the tool never runs and the model is told it failed** | **VERIFIED (positive control); unreachable under enforce since Bundle 2 (`--tree-walk` is refused, exit 4: `tests/cli/test_tree_walk_enforce.sh`); open under audit and off** | Measured on `bf489f0` with the stub harness, identical fixture and config on both engines: VM `TOOL_CALLS=1 TOOL_SUCCESS=true`; `--tree-walk` `TOOL_CALLS=1 TOOL_SUCCESS=false`. The VM column is the positive control. **The failure is near-silent**: `tool_loop_exit_reason=text_response` and **exit 0** on both, and both print `CONTENT=final answer using the tool result` — the model reasons confidently around a tool result it never got. The only signal is `tool_results[i].success`, which a script must inspect deliberately. |
| F37 | `env.load_dotenv` ignores `blocked_paths` | **VERIFIED + FIXED** | same mechanism as F34, fixed with it. The no-argument form defaults to `.env`, which no engine-side gate can see — resolved and checked in `env_impl.cpp`; IE-09 is the arm that isolates it |

| F40 | dangling `g_current_interpreter` (thread-local UAF) | **VERIFIED + FIXED** | #217. Report's prescription was itself a regression — see below |

| F42 | unchecked recursive marshalling -> SIGSEGV | **VERIFIED + FIXED** | #215 |
| F43 | **SSRF filter is a STRING check on the caller's URL — it misses redirect destinations AND DNS** | **fixed** (#229) | Measured on `bf489f0` entirely locally (no external network; `/etc/hosts` already maps the non-`localhost` name `runsc` to 127.0.0.1). Two local servers: a redirector on :18431 that 302s to `http://127.0.0.1:18432/secret`, and a target on :18432 serving `LOOPBACK_SECRET_REACHED`. **A, POSITIVE CONTROL** — `http.get("http://127.0.0.1:18432/secret")` is BLOCKED, exit 1, *HTTP request to private network denied*: the filter works. **C, F43** — `http.get("http://runsc:18431/go")` follows the 302 and returns `LOOPBACK_SECRET_REACHED`, exit 0. The redirect target is the LITERAL `127.0.0.1` that arm A blocks. `isPrivateHost()` runs once, on the caller's URL; `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION=1` + `MAXREDIRS=5` then follow hops the filter never sees. `REDIR_PROTOCOLS_STR` bounds the SCHEME only, so a same-scheme hop to `169.254.169.254` is unguarded. See F47 — the same probe found a second hole that needs no redirect at all. **UPDATE — FIXED by #229 (`87c9041`).** Enforcement moved to `CURLOPT_OPENSOCKETFUNCTION`, which libcurl calls with the RESOLVED `curl_sockaddr` once per socket — the initial request AND every redirect hop — so neither a redirect, a DNS record, nor an alternate IP spelling can reach a private address. Pinned by `tests/security/test_ssrf_redirect_dns.sh` (7 arms, incl. SS-04 the over-block control and SS-05 per-notation). -- reconciled 2026-10-19: test_ssrf_redirect_dns.sh SS-03 |
| F44 | trust store: `default.pub` blind spot + injection guard | **real defect, exploitability UNPROVEN** | see below |
| F45 | `--lock-check` accepts a lockfile whose `.sig` was deleted | **NOT A DEFECT — already fixed** | see below |
| F46 | **`debug.env`/`stack`/`snapshot`/`trace`-location are tree-walker-only under the VM** — the deferred half of F35, recorded rather than carried in a PR description. All four reach the tree-walker through `g_debug_interpreter` (`src/stdlib/debug_impl.cpp` lines 317, 432, 456, 501), set by `DebugModule::setInterpreter()` from `src/interpreter/interpreter.cpp` and nowhere else, so under the VM — the DEFAULT engine — the pointer is null. Unlike `is_tainted` (F35), these need the interpreter's SCOPE and CALL STACK, which the VM does not expose through this interface: there is no engine-agnostic accessor to fall back to, so the fix is a VM introspection API, not a re-route. | **VERIFIED (positive control), fix open** | Measured on `bf489f0`, same build, same program: `debug.keys(debug.env())` → `[]` on the VM vs `["outer_var", "n"]` on `--tree-walk`; `debug.stack()` → `[]` vs a real frame list. Positive control is the tree-walk column — the calls work, so the VM result is absence, not a broken fixture. **Severity is BELOW F35 and the direction is why**: these degrade to VISIBLY EMPTY, whereas F35 returned a confident wrong answer to a security question. Nothing here is offered to scripts as a guard. Pinned by `DT-05` in `tests/governance_v4/test_debug_is_tainted_parity.sh`, which asserts the VM still returns `[]` — so a change that fixes this must update that arm deliberately instead of silently widening what the F35 suite claims. Not fixed with F35: re-routing an accessor is one line, building scope/stack introspection for the VM is not, and bundling them would have made the F35 fix unreviewable. |
| F47 | **the SSRF filter never resolves DNS, so any hostname pointing at a private IP walks through it** | **fixed** (#229) | Found while building the F43 probe, not looked for. Same fixture, same destination, different spelling of the host: **B** — `http.get("http://runsc:18432/secret")` returns `LOOPBACK_SECRET_REACHED` at exit 0, while arm A (`http://127.0.0.1:18432/secret`, the identical endpoint) is HARD-blocked. No redirect is involved. Both layers are pure string checks: `isPrivateHost()` (`http_impl.cpp`) tests the literal `localhost` then `inet_pton`, and returns false for anything that is not an IP literal; `Sandbox::canConnect()` (`sandbox.cpp`) compares `allowed_hosts` as strings and also never resolves. **Arguably more severe than F43**: it needs no redirect, no attacker-controlled server, and an attacker who controls any DNS record (or a public name like `localtest.me`) reaches `169.254.169.254` directly. **UPDATE — FIXED by #229 (`87c9041`).** Enforcement moved to `CURLOPT_OPENSOCKETFUNCTION`, which libcurl calls with the RESOLVED `curl_sockaddr` once per socket — the initial request AND every redirect hop — so neither a redirect, a DNS record, nor an alternate IP spelling can reach a private address. Pinned by `tests/security/test_ssrf_redirect_dns.sh` (7 arms, incl. SS-04 the over-block control and SS-05 per-notation). -- reconciled 2026-10-19: test_ssrf_redirect_dns.sh SS-02 |
| F48 | **the agent tool loop drops every resilience mechanism the normal send path has** | **fixed** (#236) | `agent_impl.cpp` says so itself at the retry loop: *"simplified — single attempt"*, and sets `loop_config.model = models[0]`. On `!success` it sets `tool_loop_exit_reason="api_error"` and breaks. Reviewer's own correction, worth keeping: it DOES scan keys for the first non-dead one — what it lacks is rotation AFTER a failure, model fallback, and backoff. So a tool-using agent is strictly less resilient than the same agent without tools, which is the opposite of what an operator would assume. **BLOCKED ON F54** — the tool loop does not count its calls toward the run budget, so adding retries multiplies uncounted spend. Wire the accounting first. -- reconciled 2026-10-19: the tool loop shares selectAttemptKey/retry/fallback with the send path |
| F49 | **`ToolCallInfo` is lossy — no provider-extra fields survive a tool round-trip** | reported, **structure confirmed, IMPACT UNVERIFIED** | `agent_provider.h:13-17` is exactly `{id, name, arguments}`; no `extra_json`, no signature field anywhere in the provider layer. The downstream claim (provider rejects turn 2 for a missing thought signature) **needs a live key and was not measured** — plausible for providers that sign reasoning, but do not fix on speculation. Probe it before writing code. |
| F50 | **the tool loop discards the error detail it already has** | **fixed** (#236) | `AgentResponse` carries `std::string error; // non-empty on failure`. The tool loop reads `.success`, sets the literal `"api_error"`, and never reads `.error`. The diagnostic exists and is thrown away, so a tool-loop failure is unattributable between auth, rate-limit, timeout and malformed-response. Cheapest item in the set. **FIXED** — `tool_loop_exit_reason` now carries the sanitized, 200-char-bounded provider error. Verified through the stub: a 500 on the tool loop's second call reports `api_error: Agent error: API returned status 500` where it previously reported the bare literal. Additive: it still STARTS with `api_error` so old matchers keep working (TD-02). Test: `tests/governance_v4/test_tool_loop_diagnostics.sh`, 3/3. |
| F51 | **no production tier lets a supervisor absorb a child's HARD violation** | **BY DESIGN — recorded, not a defect** | `GovernanceHardError` is intentionally uncatchable and the re-throw is enforced at every catch site in `interpreter.cpp` and `vm.cpp` (see the CLAUDE.md gotcha). The only catchable tier is DETECT, commented *"For testing."* in `governance.h:48`. Recorded rather than discarded because the ERGONOMIC cost is real and will be rediscovered: an orchestration script cannot contain a child agent's HARD block, it can only die with it. If that is ever revisited it should be a new tier with its own ratchet, never by loosening the existing re-throw. |
| F52 | **an object- or array-typed tool argument arrives as a JSON STRING, not a dict** | reported as a by-product, **not probed here** | Surfaced while DISPROVING the escaped-characters claim (see below). `json::parse(tc.arguments)` then `val.get<std::string>()` correctly unescapes scalars, but a nested object/array falls through to `val.dump()`, so a parameter declared `type: object` in the tool schema reaches the NAAb function as text. Separate defect from the one being disproven, and it would have been lost if the disproof had been recorded as a bare negative. |
| F53 | **`TelemetryForwarder`'s buffered events never reach the webhook/SIEM when the process dies on a HARD block** | **fixed 2026-10-28** (Bundle 4.1) | The reported claim (telemetry lost on fatal exit) is WRONG about the JSONL file: it is durable per event — `fopen`/`flock`/`fwrite` with an RAII deleter that `fclose`s, so nothing is pending at `_exit(3)`. The real gap is next door: `TelemetryForwarder` holds a `buffer_` deque with `batch_size` batching whose drain lives in `~TelemetryForwarder()`, which `_exit(3)` never runs. Opt-in (needs a URL configured). **Note the irony**: the tamper-chain documentation names SIEM forwarding as the mitigation for a run that dies without a `RunEnd` — the mitigation is the thing that does not survive the death it mitigates. **Fixed 2026-10-28, and wider than recorded** (observed on master with a loopback sink): a CLEAN run forwarded nothing either, because a clean run also ends in `_exit(0)`; a HARD block forwarded 1 of 3 events; and four chained events were never enqueued at all (`RunStart`, `RunEnd`, `GovernanceCheckSummary`, the end-of-run `GOVERNANCE_HEALTH_WARNING`), so even a drained stream could not be verified. Every writer now calls `forwardTelemetry()`; every CLI `_exit()` goes through `naab::exitProcess()`, which drains every forwarder within `forward_shutdown_drain_ms` (one deadline; each POST capped at the time left; an in-flight one aborted past it). The forwarded stream now verifies on its own (`--verify-telemetry-chain`). Test: `test_telemetry_forward.sh` FW-00..FW-04c (red first on master: FW-01/02/03/04; five mutants, one per hunk, each turning its arms red). Not covered: a signal kill, the REST/MCP servers; the per-POST cap and abort are exercised only when the worker is idle at the exit (traced, no arm). |
| F54 | **tool-loop API calls do not count toward `agent_dispatch.hard_stop.max_calls_per_run`** | **fixed** (#236); budgets completed by #323 (A46) | Found while tracing whether F48 was safe to do, not looked for. The tool loop READS `s_dispatch.hard_stopped` (so it honours a stop another path tripped) but never increments `s_dispatch.total_calls` — all three increment sites are outside it. Measured with `max_calls_per_run: 2`: the initial send spent one call and the tool loop then ran **3 further turns unstopped**, so at least 4 API calls under a 2-call budget. **This blocks F48** — adding retry/fallback would turn one uncounted call per loop turn into up to `max_attempts` uncounted calls, a spend-containment bypass dressed as a resilience improvement. Wire the accounting first, and prefer extracting the main path's retry loop into a shared helper over a second copy: the main loop carries two-pass key selection, throttle preference, key-offset persistence, hard-stop checks and telemetry, and a duplicate would drift — the one-key-two-matchers failure F17 avoided. |
| F55 | **VM "register aliasing" between agent handles held in the same scope** | **NOT REPRODUCED — claim withdrawn, mechanism does not exist** | Reported by an outside agent as the highest-priority item in its batch ("fix the VM register aliasing bug first"): multiple `agent.create()` handles live in one scope were said to share a VM local slot, so a send against one mutates another's state. **Five shapes, both engines, measured on `eb8438e`: no aliasing in any of them.** (1) two handles in `main`; (2) four handles in `main`; (3) two handles inside a function body; (4) three handles pushed in a `while` loop; (5) interleaved sends reading each handle's `turns_used` back out of `agent.environment()`. Shape 5 is the decisive one because it tests the MUTATION the claim predicts rather than just identity: `send(a)` gives `a=1 b=0` and the following `send(b)` gives `a=1 b=1`, on both the VM and `--tree-walk`. Shapes 1-4 show distinct `id` and `config_name` per handle. The premise is live — `MainBlock` calls `beginScope()` (`compiler.cpp:405`), so a `let` in `main` really is a local slot and not a global — but `addLocal()` (`:125`) unconditionally appends and returns `locals.size()-1`, so distinct names get distinct slots by construction, and there is no slot reuse or pooling path anywhere in the compiler. The one reuse site (`:1290`) resolves the SAME name for pre-hoisted function declarations, which is redeclaration, not aliasing between two names. `git log` shows no compiler slot fix between the reported build and `eb8438e`, so "already fixed" does not explain the negative either. **What this does NOT establish:** the reporter's SYMPTOM may still have been real — a negative on the diagnosis is not a negative on the observation — but it is not this mechanism, and the next investigation should start from the symptom rather than re-deriving the slot theory. Recorded so it is not re-investigated from scratch. Probe kept out of the suite deliberately: it pins an absence with no positive control available (there is no way to make the compiler alias two slots on purpose), and per the standing rules an absence assertion without one is not a test. |

**F43 + F47 — one defect with two faces: the check is on a STRING, the
connection is to a RESOLVED ADDRESS, and nothing reconciles them.**

`isPrivateHost()` inspects the host substring of the URL the caller passed.
Two things happen after it returns:

1. libcurl resolves that string to an address. The filter never sees the
   result, so `runsc` -> 127.0.0.1 is a clean bypass (F47).
2. libcurl follows up to 5 redirects itself (`CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION`), and
   the filter never sees those hops either (F43).

Measured, all three arms on the same build and the same target endpoint:

    A  http://127.0.0.1:18432/secret   exit 1   BLOCKED (positive control)
    B  http://runsc:18432/secret       exit 0   LOOPBACK_SECRET_REACHED
    C  http://runsc:18431/go  --302->  exit 0   LOOPBACK_SECRET_REACHED

The real-world target is `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/` — cloud
instance metadata, i.e. credentials. The probe uses loopback instead because
it proves the same thing without leaving the container, and because retrieving
an actual marker from a destination arm A blocks is a stronger claim than a
connection error to an unroutable address.

**The fix is not another string check.** Filtering redirect Location headers
would close F43 and leave F47 wide open, and re-checking the hostname would
close neither. Enforcement has to move to the point where an address is
actually known: `CURLOPT_OPENSOCKETFUNCTION` (libcurl 8.5.0 here) is called
with the RESOLVED `curl_sockaddr` before each socket is created, on every hop
including redirects, and returning `CURL_SOCKET_BAD` refuses the connection.
One hook covers both faces; `isPrivateHost()` on the URL stays as a cheap
first-pass rejection with a better error message.

Note the blast radius is bounded and worth stating: this is `http_impl.cpp`
only. Agent providers connect through `agent_provider.cpp`, which is a
separate curl setup and deliberately permits loopback `api_base` for test
stubs — so the fix does not break the stub harness.

**F35 — I shipped the wrong fix for this, and reverted it. The wrong fix is
worth recording, because it is the one anybody tracing this will reach for.**

`debug.is_tainted(name)` is a NAME lookup against `GovernanceEngine::taint_set_`.
`DebugModule::checkTainted()` reached that set through `g_debug_interpreter`,
set by `DebugModule::setInterpreter()` from `src/interpreter/interpreter.cpp`
and nowhere else — so it is null under the VM and the function returned `false`
before consulting anything.

That trace is correct, and it is not the defect. I re-routed the accessor to the
engine-agnostic `GovernanceEngine::getCurrent()`, committed it with a confident
message, and the regression test then reported no change at all. **The data is
not in `taint_set_` under the VM in the first place:**

- `OP_DEFINE_GLOBAL` / `OP_SET_GLOBAL` call `markTainted(name)` — **globals only**
- locals carry taint on `taint_stack_`, a value-stack shadow with **no name**
- sink checks call `markTainted("argument 0 of 'f()'")`, run `checkTaintedSink`,
  then `clearTaint()` it immediately (`vm.cpp`) — transient scaffolding under a
  synthetic label, never a durable record

So for any ordinary `let`, the name is never in the set under the VM, and no
accessor change can find it. A real fix needs either durable name-keyed taint
for VM locals, or `debug.is_tainted` resolving a name to a stack slot and
reading `taint_stack_`. Both are design changes to the taint model.

Two method notes, because neither was a coding error:

- **I traced to the accessor and stopped** — the point of MENTION, not the point
  of EFFECT. The question "does `taint_set_` actually contain VM locals?" was one
  level further and would have killed the fix before it was written.
- **The fix was caught only because the test was written first and run against
  the unfixed binary.** Had I written the test after building, it would have
  been shaped to pass, and a no-op change would have shipped as a fix.

**F36 — verified, and the obvious fix opens a taint hole. Read this before
"just wiring up the tree-walker."**

Tool execution funnels through `gov_engine->callVMFunction()` in
`agentSend()` (`src/stdlib/agent_impl.cpp`), gated on `hasVMCallbacks()`.
`setVMCallbacks()` is installed in exactly two places, both inside the VM
branch of `src/cli/main.cpp`, both binding `bytecode_vm.callNaabFunction`. The
tree-walker installs nothing, so `callVMFunction()` throws, and the throw is
caught by the tool loop's own `catch (const std::exception&)` — sanitized,
